Paramount Wins Brussels and Asks for a Mini-Trial as the $7M-a-Day Clock Runs
Paramount must give up its UIP stake within 13 months of closing under the EU's clearance conditions; it has now asked the court for a three-day evidentiary hearing in mid-August.
CEO Summary
The European Commission cleared Paramount Skydance's acquisition of Warner Bros. Discovery on July 22, with conditions including divestiture of Paramount's UIP stake, bringing clearances to 65 jurisdictions. The last obstacle is the 12-state antitrust suit in the US, where a 14-day TRO is in place and a preliminary-injunction hearing is set for August 3. Paramount asked the court for a three-day evidentiary hearing in mid-August, racing a $7 million per day ticking fee that starts September 30.
Why This Matters
Paramount owes Warner Bros. Discovery $7 million per day starting September 30 if the merger has not closed by then. Its own court brief states that cost could reach "well over $1 billion in ticking fees and other additional costs" if the injunction fight runs its course. Paramount has asked the court for a three-day evidentiary hearing in mid-August rather than proceed on the states' preferred schedule, a request consistent with minimizing the number of days the fee accrues.

The European Commission's clearance decision found that high-budget "blockbuster" films are not a separate relevant market and identified Amazon MGM, A24, and Lionsgate as competitors to a combined Paramount-WBD. Paramount's release argues this finding "directly refutes" the state lawsuit's market definition. The EU decision does not bind the US court, but it addresses the same market-definition question at the center of the state case.
Specific Implication
Parties with deals tied to either company should track the August 3 preliminary-injunction hearing and Paramount's request for an expedited evidentiary hearing. The ticking fee makes hearing dates, not the litigation's ultimate outcome, the near-term driver of Paramount's posture.
The Facts
EU clearanceEuropean Commission formally cleared Paramount Skydance's acquisition of Warner Bros. Discovery on July 22, with conditions — including Paramount giving up its stake in United International Pictures within 13 months of closing (per the Commission's decision, via The Verge)
Jurisdictions clearedParamount's release counts 65 jurisdictions that have cleared or declined to challenge, incl. US DOJ, Australia, Brazil, Canada, China (SAMR), South Korea, South Africa, plus foreign-direct-investment clearances in Germany, France, Spain, Italy and others
US postureA federal judge issued a 14-day temporary restraining order on Monday (7/20) and set an Aug. 3 hearing on a preliminary injunction, in the 12-state attorneys-general antitrust suit
Paramount's ask (filed 7/22)A three-day evidentiary hearing — effectively a mini-trial — the week of Aug. 17 or Aug. 24, plus consent to extend the TRO through the court's decision
The moneyAfter Sept. 30, Paramount owes WBD a $7 million per day ticking fee if the deal has not closed. Paramount's brief says an injunction would cost it "well over $1 billion in ticking fees and other additional costs"
Competing schedulesCourt's schedule: state opening brief Thursday, company response Monday, state reply July 30. Paramount proposed: openings July 28, company reply Aug. 7, state response Aug. 12
WGAFiled its own preliminary-injunction motion, seeking to align with the states on Aug. 3; Paramount called that "not feasible" but workable later in August

Paramount's release argues the Commission's findings "directly refute" the state complaint's market definition — specifically that the Commission declined to treat high-budget/"blockbuster" films as a separate relevant market, and defined film production to include "smaller US studios such as Amazon MGM, A24 and Lionsgate, as well as European studios."
· Deadline + Paramount Skydance investor release + Variety, The Wrap, The Verge (corroborators) · 2026-07-22 · https://deadline.com/2026/07/paramount-warner-bros-state-attorney-general-lawsuit-1237000888/ · Provenance: filing/transcript source ↗
WGA Puts a Number on Studio Buying Power: 35% of the Market for Writing Services on Top-Grossing Films
The WGA's filing claims a combined Paramount-WBD would hold 35% of the market for writing services on anticipated top-grossing films, above the presumptive-illegality threshold.
CEO Summary
The WGA filed for a preliminary injunction in federal court to halt the Paramount-WBD merger, separate from the 12-state suit. Its claim is a labor-market antitrust theory: the combined company would hold 35% of the market for writing services on anticipated top-grossing films, a share above the presumptive-illegality threshold. The guild wants its hearing aligned with the states' August 3 date and says it will seek its own TRO if refused.
Why This Matters
Filing separately rather than joining the states is the consequential choice. A structural settlement between Paramount and the attorneys general would resolve the consumer case without touching the guild's. The labor-market claim therefore survives any deal cut with the states, and Paramount cannot buy its way to closing through one negotiation. The merger now has to clear two independent theories of harm, and the cheaper one to settle does not retire the other.
Specific Implication
The 35% figure and the labor-market theory behind it are now in a federal court filing with a case number attached. It is available as citable language in future merger reviews involving creative-services concentration, independent of how this case resolves.
The Facts
  • The Writers Guild of America filed a motion Wednesday morning in federal court in Oakland seeking a preliminary injunction to halt Paramount's acquisition of Warner Bros. Discovery. The guild's case is separate from the 12-state attorneys general suit.
  • The WGA's core claim is a labor-market antitrust theory, not a consumer one: the transaction is "presumptively illegal in the market for writing services for anticipated top grossing films," where the combined company would hold a 35% share.
  • The guild argues irreparable harm because a completed merger would be "virtually impossible to unwind," rendering any divestiture remedy ineffective, and cites "lost employment opportunities, suppressed compensation, and narrowed creative possibilities."
  • On the balance of equities, the WGA argues any harm from a delayed closing is "self-inflicted" — a reference to Paramount's own deal terms, which carry a $7 million per day ticking fee for every day the merger is not closed past Sept. 30.
  • The WGA is asking the court to align its hearing with the state AGs' Aug. 3 preliminary-injunction hearing. Paramount's attorneys "could not secure their clients' consent" to that schedule. Absent an expedited schedule, the WGA says it "would be forced" to seek its own TRO.
  • Sequence set by the court: state AG PI motion due Thursday, Paramount opposition Monday, state reply July 30.
  • Paramount's position, stated when the WGA suit was filed: a combined company "will have the scale and resources to reverse the current trends in our industry and expand opportunities for writers, not shrink them: more development slates, more series and film greenlights."
· Deadline · Ted Johnson · 2026-07-22 · https://deadline.com/2026/07/writers-guild-paramount-warner-bros-lawsuit-1237000083/ · Provenance: filing/transcript source ↗
A Hot Package Got Passed On Because of a Franchise Conflict, Not the Material — and Landed at a Rival
The source novel has sold 100M+ copies in 50+ languages, and Disney's own 2002 animated adaptation is why 20th Century passed rather than compete with Pirates of the Caribbean.
CEO Summary
Sony Pictures is in negotiations to acquire a pirate-genre feature package built on a public-domain adventure novel, with a top-tier director attached and a movie star in the lead villain role. The director's home studio, 20th Century, had first look and passed, per Deadline because Pirates of the Caribbean is a priority at parent Disney and the company did not want a competing pirate project. Sony pursued it from the start, driven by its studio head's long relationship with the director.
Why This Matters
The public-domain status of the 1883 source novel exposes the flaw in the pass. Because anyone can adapt the material, declining it did not prevent a competing pirate film from existing. It only determined which studio finances one. Disney protected its franchise lane on paper while handing a rival the director, the star, and the produced screenplay. A portfolio-conflict pass on public-domain material therefore buys no defensive value at all, and studios applying franchise-conflict logic to unownable IP are ceding packages rather than protecting lanes.
Specific Implication
A seller shopping a package should map where a buyer's franchise portfolio creates a conflict before submission, and should treat executives' prior-studio relationships as live routes to a sale alongside current first-look obligations.
The Facts
The packageA pirate-genre feature adaptation of a public-domain 19th-century adventure novel, with a top-tier feature director attached and a movie star in the lead villain role, from a produced screenplay by a well-regarded adapter
Where it landedSony Pictures is in negotiations to acquire it. Sony declined comment
The first look20th Century — the director's usual studio home, releasing his next film in August — got first look and passed
Why it passedNot the material and not the relationship: 20th is a Disney division, and Pirates of the Caribbean is a priority at Disney's live-action unit, so Disney did not want a competing pirate project and let it go to market
Why Sony wonSony "was high on it from the jump," which Deadline ties to the director's long-standing relationship with Sony's studio head — who championed the same director's films while co-chairman of 20th Century, and backed two of his more recent pictures
TimelineDeadline first reported the package last month, after the director declared it his next project
Underlying IPThe source novel (1883) has sold 100M+ copies in 50+ languages; a prior animated adaptation was released by Disney in 2002

A Filmmaker-Owned AI Studio Just Raised $11M of a $15M Round — Outside the Studio System
The company's SEC filing shows just over $11M of a $15M round already sold as of July 1, funding a slate built after its AI shorts drew public backlash last winter.
CEO Summary
An AI film and content studio founded in 2025 by an Oscar-nominated feature director has filed with the SEC for a $15 million equity raise, with just over $11 million already sold as of early July. The company holds a non-exclusive filmmaking partnership with Google DeepMind and is hiring a Head of Studio to oversee a slate spanning creator content, features, television, and branded content.
Why This Matters
The structure inverts the studio-AI partnerships in the comparison set. A24 and Lionsgate rent AI capability from model companies, while here the filmmaker owns the entity, keeps the model relationship non-exclusive, and sells equity to outside investors. Whatever appreciating asset AI-driven production creates accrues to the talent rather than to a studio, and the financing ran through securities markets with no studio in the capital stack. That is a disintermediation of the studio's traditional financing role executed at the level of a single filmmaker.
Specific Implication
A filmmaker structuring an outside equity raise for an AI-driven studio, rather than taking a partnership from a model provider or a traditional studio, should secure clear terms on ownership of model outputs. Equity protections tied to continued founder involvement belong in the same negotiation, before those terms are set by precedent.
The Facts
The entityAn AI film and content studio founded in 2025 by an Oscar-nominated feature director with a producing partner, both listed as executive officers
The raiseAn SEC filing earlier in July 2026 for a $15 million equity raise; the document states just over $11 million already sold, first sale July 1
Use of fundsNot stated in the filing. The company's site lists open roles for "generative artists," social media strategists, a chief of staff, and a "Head of Studio" who will "oversee the development, production and commercialization of our slate, including digital-first creator content, feature films, television series, and branded content"
Model partnershipA non-exclusive filmmaking partnership with Google DeepMind, under which the company produced a 45-minute featurette (first of a planned trilogy) that premiered at Tribeca in 2025
Prior outputA set of American Revolution-themed shorts using AI generations of historical figures, premiered winter 2025-26 to significant public backlash; pivoted last month to an animated approach
Comparison setTHR notes other established filmmakers using AI for storyboarding and animated development, and studios including A24 and Lionsgate having forged creative partnerships with AI companies

YouTube Books an $11B Ad Quarter and 1.7B World Cup Viewers — the Scale Number Every Streamer Is Now Measured Against
Alphabet raised its full-year capex guidance to $195-205B from $180-190B, while Google Cloud alone grew 82% to roughly $25B in the quarter.
CEO Summary
Alphabet reported Q2 revenue of $119.8B, up 24%, with YouTube ad revenue topping $11 billion, up 13% year over year. Over 1.7 billion unique viewers watched World Cup-related videos on YouTube during the tournament, and Alphabet raised full-year capex guidance to $195-205B.
Why This Matters
The structural point sits on the cost side the results do not itemize. YouTube pays for programming through revenue shares after the audience shows up, while every streamer it is measured against pays license fees and production costs before. A quarter delivered off content the platform neither commissioned nor licensed, including a global sports event whose broadcast rights sat elsewhere, shows rights ownership and audience capture decoupling. The entity holding the rights is no longer guaranteed the larger share of the attention.
Specific Implication
A bidder valuing exclusive live rights should discount the exclusivity premium for the related-programming audience that YouTube captures without holding the rights, a pool the tournament put at over 1.7 billion unique viewers.
The Facts
YouTube ad revenue, Q2 2026More than $11 billion, up 13% year over year
Alphabet total revenue$119.8B, up 24% YoY
Google advertising (Search + YouTube + Network)$81.6B, of which Search alone was $63.3B
Google CloudRoughly $25B, growing 82%
Subscriptions$12.9B
EPS$9.11 — nearly quadrupled; beat consensus
World CupOver 1.7 billion unique viewers watched World Cup-related videos on YouTube during FIFA World Cup 2026, per Alphabet's prepared remarks
Capex guidanceFull-year 2026 capital expenditure raised to $195–205B from $180–190B (per The Information)

Verification note: THR's copy states Alphabet's "Q2 profit beyond $112 billion." That figure is not consistent with the $119.8B total-revenue and $9.11 EPS figures in the same paragraph and is almost certainly a transcription error for a revenue line. It is not carried as a fact here.

A Federal Production Incentive Now Has Republican Sponsors and a Studio CEO Lobbying for It
Paramount Skydance spent $2.86M lobbying in 2026, and its CEO visited Washington July 13, the same day states filed to block the $111B WBD merger.
CEO Summary
A stackable federal production incentive, layered on top of state credits to compete with Australia and Canada, now has named Republican sponsors in the House alongside longtime Democratic backers, plus support from the major unions, the studios, and the administration's industry ambassador. Paramount Skydance has spent $2.86 million lobbying in 2026, and its CEO went to Washington July 13 to court Republican committee members. The blocker is a coordination standoff: members want presidential cover, the White House wants a legislative vehicle.
Why This Matters
The Republican roster maps to production geography, not ideology: Rep. Jack's district includes Trilith Studios, and Rep. Hern's argument is Oklahoma's 22,000 jobs since 2021. The credit's constituency has become the districts that already host soundstages and crews, which is what makes it durable. Incentives with geographic constituencies behave like farm subsidies once enacted. The same geography also predicts the design: a bill carried by Georgia, Oklahoma, Texas and Ohio members will be shaped to keep feeding those hubs, not to repatriate production to California.
Specific Implication
If a federal production incentive is enacted, who captures its value, studio, financier, or a talent-controlled production entity, becomes a negotiable deal term, as state credits already are.
The Facts
The mechanismA stackable federal incentive layered on top of existing state credits for film and TV shot in the U.S., to compete with Australia and Canada, which already do this
The lobbyingParamount Skydance has spent $2.86 million on lobbying so far in 2026 (House disclosures), covering "issues related to federal film/TV production incentives" among other areas; meetings with Senate, House, FCC and Treasury staff
The visitParamount Skydance's chief executive went to D.C. July 13 to court Republican members of a key House committee — the same day the states filed to block the $111B Paramount-WBD merger
Republican backers namedRep. Brian Jack (GA — district includes Trilith Studios) as a key backer alongside longtime Democratic sponsor Rep. Laura Friedman (CA); also Reps. Nicole Malliotakis (NY), Nathaniel Moran (TX), Mike Carey (OH), Kevin Hern (OK)
On the recordMalliotakis: "There is a growing coalition of actors, of labor unions, of film production companies and a bipartisan group of members that want to see something happen by the end of the year." Hern cites Oklahoma's industry having "created over 22,000 jobs since 2021"
The coalitionHollywood's top unions and studios, the administration's special ambassador to the industry, and independent filmmakers. An economic impact study is close to publication; a D.C. "day of advocacy" is under discussion
The blockerThe executive director of the Coalition for American Production: "Members of Congress are waiting for the president's OK to go ahead and sponsor this. Meanwhile, the White House is waiting for them to propose a vehicle, so it's a little bit of a standoff"
VehicleUndecided — a tax package, the next reconciliation package, or a standalone bill. Target committee: House Ways and Means
SenateLess clear. Sen. Adam Schiff still pushing; Paramount's CEO is said to have met some Democratic senators about a bipartisan approach
The skepticismLabor sources doubted the CEO's impact and called him "a controversial messenger" mid-merger, while conceding his White House relationship "can't hurt"

The New Development Path: Make It for $1,000, Post It to Instagram, Then Take the Meeting
Two writers made a 12-episode comedy for $1,000 total and drew 1.5M views in under three months; one creator with 530,000 followers is turning down Hollywood offers.
CEO Summary
Two writers stuck in TV development made a 12-episode buddy comedy for $1,000, posted it to Instagram, drew roughly 1.5M views in under three months, and now have brands offering to fund a second season. The Ankler counts more than two dozen vertically-shot scripted comedies in the space, plus a fully financed six-episode indie season made with no distributor attached and a brand-funded workplace comedy with 530,000+ followers whose owner is declining Hollywood offers.
Why This Matters
The detail that matters most is in the Facts: the writers reconfigured their scripts because the Instagram algorithm favors videos under three minutes. The recommendation engine is performing the development executive's function, shaping structure, pacing and episode length before any buyer sees the material. What arrives at a studio as audience-proven has therefore already been creatively optimized for platform retention rather than for television. Buyers treating these shows as de-risked development are inheriting a format decision they did not make, and the conversion cost back to conventional episode shape is the unpriced part of every such acquisition.
Specific Implication
A creator with an established, revenue-generating audience on a self-produced show is positioned to negotiate a studio offer as an acquisition of an existing asset with a working revenue line, rather than as a development deal.
The Facts
The case studyTwo writers stuck in TV development made a 12-episode buddy comedy for $1,000 total, on a camera one of them already owned, and posted it to Instagram. ~1.5M views in under three months; brands have approached about funding a second season
Scale of the trendA writer-producer tracking vertically-shot scripted comedies puts the count "above two dozen" and says "the space is accelerating rapidly"
The financed modelA production company run by a former head of comedy and drama at a major streamer won a social-first series away from a major studio by fully financing a six-episode first season with no distributor attached. It was made cheaply enough that direct distribution on YouTube and social remains a viable fallback that could still recoup
Benchmark citedThat series' pilot: 260,000+ YouTube views. Comparison from a streaming launch: a well-known comedy original averaged 800,000 viewers for its first five episodes after 11 days (per a third-party analysis)
Why Instagram over YouTubeRecommendation algorithms plus short-form focus — cheaper to produce than the long-form volume YouTube's watch-time economics demand. Only a handful of creators do scripted at scale on YouTube
Format constraintThe writers reconfigured their scripts because the Instagram algorithm favors videos under three minutes
Entry bar"You don't want to start a show with less than 100,000 followers," per one comedy creator
The monetized versionA three-year-old Instagram workplace comedy: 530,000+ followers, weekly episodes, ~6 crew plus ~6 in post, entirely funded by brand deals. Its owner-creator has declined inbound Hollywood offers: "I own [the show] … To give that up, it would have to be the right opportunity"
Rep-side readThe co-CEO of a digital management firm: "A lot of people are trying to figure out how they can de-risk content spend by leaning into built-in audience"
· The Ankler — Like & Subscribe · 2026-07-22 · https://theankler.com/instragram-scripted-tv-boom-is-just-getting-started-outpacing-youtube/ · Provenance: reported-named-sources source ↗
"The Balance of Power Has Shifted": A Directors Ranking Whose Premise Is the Story
The list's implicit top benchmark is a director whose latest movie started selling tickets a year ahead of release, per Vulture's ranking a year in the making.
CEO Summary
Vulture published The 100 Most Powerful Directors in Hollywood, a year in the making, with the thesis that the buzziest deals now belong to auteurs rather than the franchise producers who dominated a decade ago. A leading analyst is quoted on the record: "The balance of power has shifted," crediting film-fan communities built around filmmakers. The implicit benchmark for the top of the list is a director whose latest movie started selling tickets a year ahead of time.
Why This Matters
The piece's own historical analogy is the caution it does not draw out. It likens today's cinephiles bristling at interconnected comic-book worlds to their forebearers in the '70s rejecting old-fashioned musicals, which frames auteur ascendancy as a generational taste cycle, not a permanent settlement. Deals built to lock in auteur-premium terms for a decade, first-dollar gross, retained derivatives, control provisions, are being priced at the top of a cycle the source material itself describes as cyclical. The durable asset is the individual director's audience, which can churn like any audience, not the auteur category.
Specific Implication
Per the analyst quoted in the piece, a filmmaker with a demonstrated, portable audience has a stronger basis than a decade ago to negotiate retained rights and derivative participation. That audience is tied to the individual rather than to studio-controlled IP.
The Facts
  • Vulture published The 100 Most Powerful Directors in Hollywood, a year in the making, with an explicit thesis in the deck: "10 years ago, franchise producers dominated the movies. Now, the buzziest deals belong to auteurs."
  • The framing argument: the industry is exiting the cinematic-universe era, when mega-producers "ruled the land," and "directors seem to matter again."
  • Cause identified: a generation of cinephiles "raised on YouTube, Reddit, and Letterboxd" who came of age under MCU hegemony and now "bristle at interconnected comic-book worlds the same way their forebearers in the '70s did at old-fashioned musicals."
  • Talent-side evidence cited: the multi-year role preparation of a marquee young screen star went to an auteur project, "not a superhero."
  • A leading industry analyst, quoted directly: "The balance of power has shifted." Previously a "big-time commercial director" was considered far more important than any Oscar-friendly auteur. "But because of the rise of film-fan culture, the communities built around filmmakers, that has given more power to the auteurs who can deliver an audience."
  • The implicit benchmark for the top of the list: "the person whose latest movie started selling tickets a year ahead of time."
· Vulture / New York Magazine · Nate Jones · 2026-07-21 · https://www.vulture.com/article/100-most-powerful-directors-in-hollywood.html · Provenance: analyst-inference source ↗
News Corp Countersues Over the Middleman: Scraping and Reselling Content to AI Companies
News Corp seeks statutory damages of up to $150,000 per infringement; Brave sued first, in March 2025, seeking a ruling that its use of the articles is fair use.
CEO Summary
News Corp countersued Brave Software in federal court in Oakland, accusing it of scraping Wall Street Journal and New York Post articles and reselling them to AI companies, and seeking an injunction plus statutory damages of up to $150,000 per infringement. Brave had sued first, in March 2025, seeking a declaratory judgment that bundling licensable articles is fair use. The suits follow failed licensing negotiations between the two.
Why This Matters
Because both sides first tried and failed to reach a "fair, market-based agreement," the litigation is functionally a pricing dispute referred to a court. A fair-use ruling for Brave sets the license price of scraped articles at zero for the entire intermediary layer, making resellers the cheapest legal channel for training data. A News Corp win makes that channel uneconomic and forces AI companies back into direct publisher licenses. Either way the outcome sets the floor under every content-for-AI negotiation publishers are currently running, which is why the case matters well beyond its parties.
Specific Implication
News Corp's market-foreclosure argument, that an unlicensed reseller channel removes the incentive to pay original content sources, is now filed as court language other publishers could cite in similar AI-licensing disputes over text content.
The Facts
  • News Corp filed a countersuit Tuesday in federal court in Oakland against Brave Software, accusing it of "flagrant theft" for distributing and selling versions of Wall Street Journal and New York Post articles to AI companies.
  • News Corp says Brave's "covert scraping" and resale fall "nowhere near the bounds" of fair use. It seeks an injunction, unspecified monetary damages, and statutory damages of up to $150,000 per infringement.
  • The core economic claim, quoted from the filing: "The more content Brave copies and sells, the more revenue it generates, and the less incentive AI companies have to negotiate licenses with the publishers who produced the content. Brave profits while publishers are cut out."
  • Procedural posture is inverted: Brave sued first, in March 2025, seeking a declaratory judgment that bundling licensable copyrighted articles is not infringement. It filed a revised complaint in May 2026 after what News Corp calls failed negotiations for a "fair, market-based agreement."
  • Brave's defense: indexing to make content searchable, and serving users snippets and "high-level summaries," is fair use. Brave has also argued News Corp is threatening to disrupt generative AI advances.
  • News Corp's chief executive said Brave's conduct shows "blatant disregard," adding: "This era of tacky tech trafficking must come to an end if journalism is to have a sustainable future."
  • The New York Post, Dow Jones, and News Corp's British and Australian operations are also defendants in Brave's suit.
· Reuters (Jonathan Stempel), via The Star · 2026-07-22 (filed July 21) · https://www.thestar.com.my/tech/tech-news/2026/07/22/news-corp-countersues-brave-for-allegedly-039scraping039-articles-for-ai- · Provenance: filing/transcript source ↗
IATSE Organizes a $90M Warner Bros. Animated Tentpole in Louisiana, 84-35
Workers voted 84-35 to unionize a 137-person unit on the $90M project, and IATSE is now demanding Warner Bros. itself, not just the vendor, reach an agreement.
CEO Summary
Creatives at Swaybox Studios, the New Orleans shop producing the $90 million Dynamic Duo for Warner Bros. Pictures Animation and DC Studios, voted 84-35 to join IATSE, creating a unit of 137 animators, digital puppetry artists and CG modelers. IATSE called it one of its largest recent organizing drives and publicly demanded that Swaybox and Warner Bros. reach an industry-standard agreement.
Why This Matters
The significant thing is what got organized: a unit built around the proprietary "Momo" technique blending CGI, stop-motion and puppetry, job categories no existing craft agreement contemplated. The vote establishes that a novel pipeline does not create a jurisdiction-free zone, and the union reached the workers before the technique's job classifications could harden outside its contracts. That sequencing matters increasingly as AI-assisted pipelines generate new crew categories faster than agreements can be renegotiated: the precedent here is organize the technique early, not grieve it later.
Specific Implication
A studio budgeting a project through a non-union regional vendor should price in the possibility of mid-schedule organization, with the union's resulting demand reaching the hiring studio rather than stopping at the vendor.
The Facts
  • Creatives at Swaybox Studios, a New Orleans-based studio producing Dynamic Duo for Warner Bros. Pictures Animation and DC Studios, voted to join IATSE in an election concluding Tuesday. The tally: 84 for, 35 against.
  • Absent valid challenges, a unit of 137 workers — animators, digital puppetry artists, CG modelers and others — joins the union.
  • IATSE says the film is budgeted at $90 million and calls the campaign "one of the largest organizing drives in film and television production undertaken by IATSE in recent years."
  • Dynamic Duo is being made with Swaybox's proprietary "Momo animation" technique — a blend of CGI, stop-motion and live-action performance using puppets.
  • IATSE Local 478 business agent Simonette Berry: workers sought "a voice on the job," union-standard wages and benefits, and changes to working conditions, adding that "productions coming to Louisiana to build world-class films can't expect to succeed by lowering standards."
  • IATSE international VP Mike Miller called on Swaybox and Warner Bros. to "come to an industry standard agreement immediately."
  • Swaybox was founded in 2014; its prior credits of note are an NFL commercial and a canceled DC project. THR sought comment from Swaybox and WB Pictures Animation.
· The Hollywood Reporter · THR staff · 2026-07-22 · https://www.hollywoodreporter.com/business/business-news/workers-dynamic-duo-studio-swaybox-unionize-1236653489/ · Provenance: named-exec-quote source ↗
A 100-Title Microdrama Slate: Vertical Video Buys Volume the Way Studios Once Bought Output
The 100-title pact lands two months after Byron Allen's BuzzFeed acquisition closed, and comes alongside plans to spin BuzzFeed Studios into a standalone entity.
CEO Summary
BuzzFeed Studios and vertical-video firm MuVpix will produce more than 100 titles over the next two years, starting with Romeo & Juliet, Reimagined in August, shot in both horizontal and vertical formats for distribution across TikTok, Reels, Shorts, FAST/CTV and MuVpix's own platform. The pact lands two months after Byron Allen's acquisition of BuzzFeed closed, with plans to spin BuzzFeed Studios into a standalone entity.
Why This Matters
The slate math only works because the inputs cost nothing to clear: the launch title is public-domain Shakespeare, and each production is shot once in both orientations to serve every platform. At a pace approaching a title a week, development stops being a selection function and becomes throughput. That reveals the real product: distribution capacity to fill vertical-video shelf space across platforms, not a library whose individual titles are expected to matter. Buyers and talent should read the commitment as an output deal for volume, priced accordingly.
Specific Implication
In a microdrama or vertical-video deal, credit, clip rights, and commerce revenue terms are not standardized and should be negotiated explicitly, since no guild framework currently governs the format.
The Facts
  • BuzzFeed Studios and vertical-video firm MuVpix have partnered to produce more than 100 titles over the next two years, launched with a cross-platform distribution strategy.
  • First title: Romeo & Juliet, Reimagined, launching in August, transplanting the Montague-Capulet feud into the modern-day Los Angeles criminal underworld. Shot in both horizontal and vertical formats.
  • MuVpix launched in March 2026 as the vertical entertainment platform of BlackForge Distribution, with two originals (Whispervale, Swipe Left), adding Romeo & Juliet, Reimagined in June.
  • The stated model: co-create original IP, reimagined classics, and "shoppable" formats built "natively for TikTok, Instagram Reels, YouTube Shorts, FAST/CTV platforms and the muVpix Originals platform."
  • BuzzFeed contributes vertical storytelling operations, production infrastructure, marketing and "cultural reach"; MuVpix contributes platform, distribution and tech.
  • MuVpix's founder: the partnership brings "premium storytelling to a format that audiences are already showing up for in massive numbers… at a scale and quality level the space hasn't seen before."
  • Corporate context: the pact comes ~two months after Byron Allen closed a deal to acquire BuzzFeed. The plan is to split BuzzFeed Studios and Tasty into independent entities, remaining wholly-owned subsidiaries.
  • BuzzFeed Studios is also attached to conventional features. Deadline calls this its biggest microdrama play to date.

California's Next Incentive Fight Is About Post, Not Principal Photography
The proposed 35% credit would for the first time pay productions that shoot elsewhere but finish post in California; it faces a Senate Appropriations hearing on August 3.
CEO Summary
AB 2319, California's proposed 35 percent postproduction tax credit, was left out of Gov. Newsom's final budget and must now secure funding through a trailer bill, with a Senate Appropriations hearing set for August 3. SAG-AFTRA's president intervened directly this week, urging members to lobby for it. The bill would, for the first time, pay productions that shoot elsewhere but finish in California, and its coalition already includes Netflix, Apple, the Editors Guild and the California IATSE Council.
Why This Matters
Netflix and Apple lining up behind a credit that rewards finishing in California what was shot somewhere else is the tell: the state's largest buyers are lobbying to subsidize the runaway model, not reverse it. Severing post eligibility from the principal-photography requirement in current law concedes that the shoots are not coming back and redraws California's defensive line around the finishing layer. An incentive architecture that follows work category by category after it has already left is managing decline. Each concession sets the precedent for the next one when visual effects or virtual production faces the same arithmetic.
Specific Implication
If enacted, the 35% postproduction credit would become a negotiable factor in where postproduction work, including ADR and voice work, is located on a project shot outside California.
The Facts
  • Gov. Gavin Newsom's final state budget, unveiled June 29, did not include funding for AB 2319, California's proposed postproduction tax credit. The bill survives and now must secure funding via a trailer bill.
  • AB 2319, carried by Assemblymember Nick Schultz, would offer a 35 percent base tax credit to productions that locate postproduction work in California but shoot elsewhere, or that shoot in California without receiving a production credit. Under current law, post expenses qualify only if principal photography also occurs in-state.
  • SAG-AFTRA's president released a video to THR ahead of a California Post Alliance (CAPA) town hall held Tuesday night on the Sony lot, urging members to lobby their representatives. This is the union's first top-officer intervention; SAG-AFTRA had filed a written letter of support earlier in the month.
  • The union's stated ask: the $750 million production tax credit was a win, but "now we have to incentivize companies to keep postproduction work in California."
  • Existing coalition backing the bill: CAPA (sponsor), the Editors Guild (co-sponsor since June), L.A. Mayor Karen Bass, the California IATSE Council, Netflix and Apple.
  • The bill has passed the state Assembly and enters the Senate Appropriations Committee on August 3, in what THR characterizes as a cash-strapped budget year.

BAFTA Gives Itself Audit Power Over AI Use — Without Banning It
BAFTA sets no ban and no disclosure threshold; it can request documentation of AI use and human involvement case by case, with the film committee ruling on eligibility.
CEO Summary
BAFTA published its 2027 film awards rules with new AI language: the awards recognize "outstanding human achievement" regardless of the tools used, and BAFTA reserves the right to demand documentation of AI use and the extent of human creative involvement, with the film committee ruling on eligibility. There is no ban, no threshold, and no mandatory declaration at entry. Separately, casting directors will now vote in performance-category longlisting and nominations, and the Outstanding British Film longlist expands to 20.
Why This Matters
Reserving the right to "dig in a bit deeper" without publishing a threshold is the shrewd part of the design. Because no producer knows what triggers a request, every film must document its AI use and human creative involvement as if it will be asked. BAFTA gets a de facto universal documentation requirement without writing a rule that could be gamed at the margin or challenged as arbitrary, and it keeps full discretion over which films to examine. Audit power held in reserve disciplines behavior more broadly than any bright-line disclosure rule would.
The Facts
  • BAFTA published eligibility and voting changes for the 2027 film awards, characterized by Variety as small tweaks relative to AMPAS's recent Oscar changes.
  • The AI language. Having added "human achievement" to the rulebook last year, BAFTA has now added: the awards recognize "outstanding human achievement in originating, shaping and realising the final artistic outcome, regardless of the tools used to bring this work to life. Where the use of Generative AI or other digital tools is relevant to the consideration of the work, BAFTA reserves the right to request additional information regarding the nature of such use and the extent of human creative involvement."
  • BAFTA's executive director of awards and content said the addition gives BAFTA the "opportunity to just dig in a bit deeper if there's something we want to check," with the film committee ruling on eligibility. Asked whether a fully AI-generated film or performance could ever be nominated: it "would always be a human that picks up a BAFTA."
  • Outstanding British Film: longlist expands from 15 to 20 after round one; nominations stay at 10 (five by membership vote, five by jury).
  • Performance categories: BAFTA's casting chapter will now vote alongside the acting chapter in longlisting and nominations, to "increase the expertise" across 700+ candidates in four categories.
  • BAFTA is not following the Oscars in permitting multiple nominations for one performer in the same category — previously allowed, banned after a review, and retained as banned to "level the playing field."
  • Bullying and harassment: CIISA-alignment declarations remain mandatory for British categories; BAFTA now requests the information from all submitted productions, with a stated expectation it becomes a requirement in future years.
  • Key dates: entries open July 22; round one voting Dec. 4; longlists Jan. 7; nominations open Jan. 19; final voting Jan. 26; ceremony Feb. 21.
· Variety · Alex Ritman · 2026-07-22 · https://variety.com/2026/film/awards/bafta-tweaks-film-awards-rules-updates-ai-guidance-1236817925/ · Provenance: named-exec-quote source ↗
A Broadway Musical Grossing $1M a Week Is Now a Money-Loser — and Only Five New Ones Are Set for Next Year
Cats: The Jellicle Ball capitalized at up to $18M against Hadestown's recouped $11.5M and an arriving technical show capitalized up to $31M; only five new musicals are set for next year.
CEO Summary
Cats: The Jellicle Ball, the Tony-winning ballroom-competition revival, announced an August 8 closing after roughly five months on Broadway despite grosses hovering around $1 million a week and high attendance. The problem was average ticket price against elevated operating costs, not demand. Only five new musicals have been announced for next year.
Why This Matters
The mismatch in the Facts is the diagnosis: the cost drivers are collective, union rates, theater rent, materials inflation, while every producer response cited is individual, smaller casts, less automation, longer marketing runways. Individual retrenchment cannot move a collectively set cost base; it only lowers the ambition of what gets staged, which is how a market that can still fill seats ends up with a shrinking pipeline. Until owners, producers and the unions negotiate the cost structure jointly, as the composer's public letter urges, the economics will keep selecting for star vehicles and recognizable IP by default. That is the exact conditioning one producer in the piece identifies as unsustainable.
Specific Implication
With fewer new stage productions in the pipeline and cost pressure on shows without a star or established title, capture and screen rights on a successful production carry standalone value. Those rights should be negotiated as their own asset rather than included by default.
The Facts
  • Cats: The Jellicle Ball — the critically acclaimed revival restaging the musical as a queer ballroom competition — announced an Aug. 8 closing after roughly five months on Broadway. It won three Tony Awards but missed Best Revival.
  • Grosses were hovering around $1 million a week pre-Tonys, with high attendance. The company was told at a July 13 meeting that the show could not generate enough revenue to cover expenses.
  • THR's reporting locates the problem not in attendance but in average ticket price: the show could fill seats, but not at prices sufficient to cover elevated Broadway operating costs.
  • Capitalization comps in the piece: Cats: The Jellicle Ball up to $18M (described as relatively low for this era); Hadestown $11.5M, recouped, now in year seven; Paddington, a technical show arriving in spring, capitalized up to $31M.
  • Only five new musicals have been announced for next year.
  • Cost drivers cited: post-pandemic supply-chain and materials inflation, utilities, theater rent, and rising rates for actors, musicians, stagehands and creative teams. Musicals are hit hardest — larger casts plus an orchestra — while plays increasingly carry celebrities who can command higher prices.
  • A lead producer on a long-running Tony-winning musical, quoted: "I'm watching a lot of shows not quite find the audience that can sustain them… There have always been more shows that fail than succeed. I think the proportion right now feels distorted."
  • The show's composer wrote publicly on July 14: "Without action, Broadway risks rivaling Hollywood's empty soundstages: increasingly dark theatres where bold new work once lived," calling on theater owners, producers and union leaders to convene.
  • Producer responses so far are individual, not collective: fewer set pieces, less automation, smaller casts, longer marketing runways, discounted previews. One incoming musical's lead producer: "The amount of time that you have for your show to pick up has shortened."
  • On audience conditioning: "We've trained audiences that the shows of value are the ones with stars and recognizable IP, and so now we have to try to dig ourselves out of that behavior pattern because that's not sustainable either."

A Franchise Its Creators Lost Two Decades Ago Came Back — and Is Now Monetizing a Second Window
The director's cut runs 13 minutes longer with over 30 minutes of bonus features; the creators lost the rights in an early-2000s dispute with Miramax's then-head and have since recovered them.
CEO Summary
The extended director's cut of Scary Movie, a Paramount and Miramax release, hit PVOD July 22, running 13 minutes longer than the theatrical cut with all-new scenes plus over 30 minutes of bonus features. The franchise's original creators lost the rights in a dispute in the early 2000s and have since recovered them; the director called the film "back with the original guys who created the franchise."
Why This Matters
The all-new scenes are what turn this from a repackaging into a template: the recovered rights are being used to manufacture new product against the old title, which is where reversion actually pays. A clawed-back franchise held passively is a trophy. The play demonstrated here treats each window as a distinct product carrying newly produced material, so the value of getting the rights back compounds with every release rather than resting on the original's catalog performance. That reframes what reversion clauses are worth negotiating for: not the library, but the license to keep making.
Specific Implication
New material produced for a second-window release, such as scenes shot specifically for a director's cut, should have its credit and compensation terms clarified separately from the terms governing the original theatrical release.

Paramount's 28-Day Window: Settle, Fight, or Walk Away From $111B
Walking away costs Paramount a $7B break fee; fighting runs about $650M a quarter in ticking fees against an $80B combined debt load.
CEO Summary
With the TRO in place, Paramount has three exits from the $111B WBD deal: settle, fight, or walk. Walking costs a $7B break fee. Fighting runs a ticking fee of roughly $7M a day, about $650M a quarter, starting Q4, against a combined $80B debt load, and a trial timeline like AT&T-Time Warner's would stack roughly $3B in fees. California's AG says he is "not interested in behavioral remedies."
Why This Matters
The attorney general's stated refusal of behavioral remedies removes the cheapest exit from the board. Consent-decree conduct promises are how prior media mega-mergers bought closure. If the only settlement on offer is structural, Paramount is no longer negotiating over promises but over which assets it divests. The economics of settling then begin to converge with the economics of losing at trial.
Specific Implication
A counterparty in active negotiations with Paramount while the ticking fee accrues should weigh that Paramount has a financial incentive to close deals quickly rather than negotiate over an extended period.

A Written Marketing Floor and a Guaranteed Window: What It Actually Took to Move a Top-Tier Director Between Studios
Universal's winning bid included a marketing floor committed in writing at parity with the $100M budget and first-dollar gross; the follow-up film was never taken to auction.
CEO Summary
Variety reconstructs the deal that moved Christopher Nolan to Universal after Warner Bros. put its 2021 slate day-and-date on streaming. Universal won the 2021 auction with a guaranteed lengthy exclusive theatrical window, a marketing floor committed in writing at parity with the $100M budget, and first-dollar gross, a reported ~$100M in fees and back end. The follow-up film was never taken to auction, and the result was a ~$265M global opening with a projected $1B run.
Why This Matters
The follow-up film was never taken to other bidders, and Donna Langley read the script at Nolan's home. The written distribution commitments bought Universal something more valuable than one picture; they bought incumbency that removed price competition from the next deal entirely. Codifying release terms in writing, not raising the cash, is what converted a one-film auction win into a relationship no rival could bid on.
Specific Implication
A guaranteed theatrical window and a marketing-spend floor tied to budget can be negotiated as written, enforceable deal terms rather than informal understandings, on the same basis as gross participation.

The Judge Grounded the Merger Pause in Theatrical Distribution, Not Cable or Streaming
The court found 27% wide-release share compelling evidence of harm, below the conventional 30% presumption threshold, and rejected streaming efficiencies as a defense in footnote 5.
CEO Summary
The court's written TRO order evaluated the Paramount-WBD merger on the market for distribution of wide-release theatrical films, not cable or streaming, and found the combined firm's 27% share "compelling evidence" of substantial market share despite sitting below the 30% presumption threshold. Footnote 5 rejected streaming-market efficiencies as a defense outright. The preliminary-injunction hearing is August 3; trial is unlikely before 2027.
Why This Matters
The order's unscrambling analysis cites consolidation of operations, sharing of business-sensitive information, and potential termination or reassignment of employees as what would make the deal impossible to unwind. That turns the merging parties' own integration planning into the evidence justifying the pause. It also sets a template: the more thoroughly two companies prepare to combine before closing, the stronger the case for stopping them. Future media mergers facing state challenges will therefore have to keep integration work frozen through litigation timelines that can run past a year.
Specific Implication
Companies that still distribute films in wide theatrical release now sit within a market a federal court has treated as legally distinct from streaming. That distinction is relevant to how any future theatrical-distributor merger is reviewed.
The Facts

The rulingA 14-day temporary restraining order on the Paramount Skydance / Warner Bros. Discovery merger.
Next datePreliminary-injunction hearing August 3. Trial unlikely before 2027.
The market namedThe court expressly evaluated the case "focusing on the market for distribution of wide-release theatrical films."
ShareCombined firm share put at 27%, below the historical 30% threshold that creates a presumption of anticompetitive effects. The court found it sufficient anyway: "compelling evidence that the combined firm... will possess substantial market share."
Streaming defense rejectedFootnote 5: the court "cannot accept Defendants' argument that the Transaction will produce efficiencies in the streaming market… Courts have expressly and repeatedly rejected the defense that a challenged merger will result in economic efficiencies ancillary to competition in the relevant market."

CEO Summary
Letterboxd is on the market at a rumored $250M ask, five times its 2023 valuation, with Paramount, Sony, Versant, RedBird, TPG, and reportedly A24 circling its 30M mostly under-35 users. The Ankler's midyear survey of dealmakers describes a K-shaped market: mega-deals and sub-$500M IP deals are clearing while the mid-market has stalled under the Paramount-WBD overhang. Dealmakers name Netflix and Amazon as likely NBCU acquirers after the mid-2027 Comcast split.
Why This Matters
The mid-market stall has a contractual expiration date buried in the Facts. The management-firm put on Lionsgate, valued at $114 million and exercisable from January 2027, means at least one mid-market seller cannot wait out the Paramount-WBD overhang indefinitely. The freeze breaks on clocks like that one, not on sentiment. The first mid-market transactions of 2027 are therefore likely to price off obligation rather than strength, setting comps below what a clear market would produce.
Specific Implication
A mid-market seller that can control its own timing should expect early-2027 comps to be set by obligated sellers such as the Lionsgate put holder, and should avoid pricing a discretionary sale off those transactions.

Brands Become TV's New Development Money — Paying Writers Fair-Market Rates As Studios Pull Back
A vertical series of 50-60 chapters costs $175,000-$225,000 to make, about ten hours of content for the price of a 30-second ad; one motorsport property booked $40M in brand deals.
CEO Summary
Consumer brands are now funding scripted development, scripts, writers, and concept work, not just non-scripted, entering pre-writing and taking producer roles. One production company has four brand-supported scripted projects including a NASCAR-set family drama for Prime Video, and an audio-equipment giant launched its own entertainment studio in June. The backdrop: streamers ordered 24% fewer scripted titles in H1 2025 than a year earlier.
Why This Matters
The norm in the Facts table is the tell. In non-scripted, brands typically fund at least half the production bill to earn a say in development. In scripted, the participation fee is negotiable and executives do not expect brand financing at tens-of-millions scale. Brands are buying the cheapest point of control in the value chain, development itself, where a modest check shapes what exists before any studio or streamer weighs in. That means brand influence over scripted content will run far ahead of brand dollars in it, and the standard tools for measuring sponsor involvement, which key off financing share, will understate it.
Specific Implication
Writers and producers evaluating brand-funded scripted development should confirm where final creative approval sits before agreeing to terms, since the brand's marketing objective enters upstream of the standard studio development process.

Locked Out of IMAX, Marvel Built Its Own Premium Format
Avengers: Doomsday took $16.5M in Infinity Vision presales in its first 24 hours, months before release, after Dune: Part Three locked up IMAX exclusivity for the same December 18 date.
CEO Summary
"Avengers: Doomsday" and "Dune: Part Three" are both holding December 18, 2026, and neither is moving. Warner Bros. secured an IMAX exclusivity deal for the date, so Disney launched Infinity Vision, its own certification-based premium format over existing exhibitor screens. Doomsday took $16.5M in Infinity Vision presales in the first 24 hours.
Why This Matters
The mechanism in the Facts is what changes the market: theaters submit their screens to Marvel for approval against laser, screen-size and sound standards. A premium format built on certification rather than proprietary hardware costs the studio nothing in capital and is replicable by any major with a tentpole slate. That undercuts the scarcity that makes an IMAX exclusivity deal worth paying for. If certification formats hold, format exclusivity stops being a weapon one studio can buy and becomes a standard every studio can mint.
Specific Implication
Participation language on films tied to premium-format release should specify that box-office uplift from a studio-branded certification format, not only from third-party formats like IMAX, is captured in the gross calculation.
The Facts
ItemDetail
The collisionAvengers: Doomsday (Marvel/Disney) and Dune: Part Three (Warner Bros.) both dated December 18, 2026. Neither is moving; Marvel's studio head confirmed the date this week.
The triggerDune secured an exclusivity deal with IMAX for the date, described as a major blow to Disney, which has historically had its pick of premium formats.
Disney's responseInfinity Vision, announced at CinemaCon: theaters submit screens to Marvel for approval; those meeting standards for laser projection, screen size and sound are certified. A premium format built from scratch.
On the recordDisney's theatrical distribution head: certification "extends that commitment to the theaters themselves, representing a shared effort between The Walt Disney Studios and the exhibition community."
Marketing postureMarvel's poster line is "12.18.26 IS DOOMSDAY." Early Infinity Vision tickets went on sale Monday, months ahead.
PresalesAvengers: Doomsday took $16.5M in the first 24 hours of Infinity Vision presales. Its trailer hit 503M views in a day, second-biggest launch ever.
The contrastUnlike the 2023 Barbenheimer pairing (a female-led comedy against an R-rated biopic), these two share near-identical target demographics.
Exhibitor viewAn Exhibitor Relations analyst: "Any number of films can survive during the holidays. It's a much different market than in the rest of the year."

CEO Summary

Thousands of Crew Flagged for Removal From the Industry Roster, With the Numbers That Explain Why
IATSE hours fell 36% since 2022 and $40M+ starts fell 37% since 2021; staying on the roster took one union day in three years, rejoining now needs 30 days in one.
CEO Summary
A few thousand IATSE members have been flagged for removal from the Industry Experience Roster, the preferred-hire list covering ~47,000 West Coast freelance crew, in the first enforcement round since 2022. Staying on required one day of union work in three years; getting back on requires 30 days in one year. The context: IATSE hours fell 36% from 2022 to 2025, and $40M+ U.S. live-action starts fell 37% from 2021 to 2025.
Why This Matters
The asymmetry between the exit and re-entry standards makes removal functionally permanent. In a market with a third fewer hours worked, thirty qualifying days in a single year is close to unattainable for the people who could not find one day in three. The roster therefore operates as a ratchet that converts a cyclical production downturn into permanent capacity loss. Because the eligibility terms were negotiated by the unions themselves with the AMPTP, the mechanism doing the converting is one IATSE signed. That is the harder problem it carries into the 2027 renegotiation than the removals themselves.
Specific Implication
A production planning a domestic shoot before the 2027 renegotiation should budget for a smaller pool of roster-listed experienced crew, since flagged members face a 30-days-in-one-year path back onto the list.
The Facts
ItemDetail
The listThe Industry Experience Roster, administered by a nonprofit founded under collective bargaining agreements since 1965. Tracks West Coast freelance crew (grips, makeup artists, plumbers) with role experience. Employers agree in union contracts to hire from it first.
Scale~47,000 currently listed (IATSE, Teamsters Local 399, LiUNA Local 724, UA Plumbers Local 78). A few thousand IATSE members flagged for removal in recent weeks.
ThresholdGenerally one day on a union project in a three-year period, here April 1 2023 to March 31 2026.
RequalificationReapply and requalify from scratch, which for many classifications means 30 days on eligible productions in one year versus the one-day-in-three that maintained status.
First since2022. Removals normally run annually but were paused post-strikes by agreement between employers and unions.
Production dataIATSE members worked ~78.9M hours in 2025, down ~36% from ~122.9M in 2022. Live-action scripted U.S. starts at $40M+ budgets fell 37% between 2021 and 2025 (ProdPro).
Union positionIATSE leadership condemns "this purging of the workforce," blames foreign tax incentives, and ties it to the push for a federal film incentive.
The bindEligibility is set by contract terms the unions themselves agreed to with the AMPTP. Next renegotiation: 2027.
ProcessFlagged members can protest; dispute resolution expected over the next few months. Several Locals filed blanket protests.

The Premium-Format Ceiling: Only 41 IMAX 70mm Screens Exist Worldwide, and the Projectors Can't Be Rebuilt
No new IMAX film projector has been built in about 50 years and IMAX no longer holds a complete manufacturing blueprint; a year of scavenging produced a net gain of just eleven screens.
CEO Summary
Only 41 IMAX 70mm locations exist worldwide, 25 in the US, for the current Universal release, and demand has some theaters sold out into the fifth week. No new IMAX film projectors have been built in about 50 years; many parts no longer exist, and IMAX no longer holds a complete manufacturing blueprint. A year of scavenging units and training 60 projectionists from scratch produced a net gain of eleven screens.
Why This Matters
The binding constraint is capital allocation, not projector scavenging. Per the Facts, exhibitors are declining to build auditoriums that can house 1.43:1 screens on construction cost, and the IMAX-AMC agreement directs new investment toward IMAX With Laser conversions instead. Scarcity in the film format is therefore being locked in by where exhibition money flows. That makes IMAX 70mm allocation a permanent, non-expandable premium that a filmmaker with sufficient leverage can contract for, and that studios will increasingly compete over as a fixed pool rather than a growing one.
Specific Implication
Screen count and hold-length commitments for IMAX 70mm engagements involve a fixed, scarce resource and can be negotiated as specific terms in a distribution agreement rather than assumed.
The Facts
ItemDetail

Worldwide IMAX 70mm locations41 for the current Universal release, up from 30 for the 2023 comparison title — a net gain of 11 (one projector was lost in the process)
Total IMAX screens worldwide~2,000
Manufacturing statusNo new IMAX film projectors built in ~50 years; existing units are retrofitted and rebuilt
PartsMany required components "simply no longer exist"; IMAX no longer holds a complete manufacturing blueprint
LaborIMAX trained 60 new projectionists from scratch
DemandSold out in some theaters into the fifth week
Constraint beyond projectorsExhibitors aren't building auditoriums capable of housing 1.43:1 screens, citing construction cost
Adjacent dealIMAX–AMC agreement last year: 12 new IMAX locations in the US, 68 existing upgraded to IMAX With Laser
NoteIMAX is not a distributor — it makes and licenses cameras and projection systems to exhibitors

The Indie Completion-Bond Market Just Went From Two Players to One
A director-led feature that premiered at Tribeca 2026 was financed, bonded, produced and post-supported entirely inside the same combined group, and is now seeking distribution.
CEO Summary
Film Finances International is acquiring Media Guarantors, combining the two largest completion-bond guarantors for independent film under one company inside the STX Entertainment and Film Services International family. The group also finances, physically produces, and posts films, and says it will invest in data-driven risk-assessment tools to speed bond issuance.
Why This Matters
A completion bond is worth paying for precisely because the guarantor is independent of the production it polices. When the sole remaining guarantor also finances, shoots and posts the same films, the instrument drifts toward self-insurance, and its certification value to outside financiers weakens accordingly. The counterweight in the Facts, a niche market where most films avoid bonding and smaller vendors are struggling, means no competitive check is likely to re-emerge on its own. What a bond certifies going forward is therefore less third-party oversight of completion risk than the group's confidence in its own pipeline.
Specific Implication
Financing arranged outside a studio should have its completion bond priced independently, without a clause tying bond issuance to using the same group's production or post-production services.
The Facts
ItemDetail
The transactionFilm Finances International, parent of Film Finances, is acquiring Media Guarantors Insurance Solutions.
OwnerThe combined company sits inside STX Entertainment and the Film Services International family.
LeadershipThe acquired company's chief executive becomes CEO of the combined bond company, under the group CEO.
TerritoriesU.S., UK, Australia, Canada, Europe.
Stated strategyA one-stop indie shop: financing, physical production, post-production, plus bonding, with accelerated investment in data-driven risk-assessment tools to speed bond issuance.
Worked exampleA director-led feature that premiered at Tribeca 2026 was financed, bonded, produced and post-supported entirely inside the group. It is currently seeking distribution.
CounterweightMost films avoid bonding if they can, so the market is niche, and smaller vendors are struggling in the current indie environment.

Public and Commercial Broadcasters Pool Streaming Inventory in Germany — Former Rivals Combining Against the Global Platforms
ProSieben carried ZDF's content without an agreement, lost in court, and is now carrying it anyway under a formal deal starting Q4 2026, free and without ads.
CEO Summary
German public broadcaster ZDF will integrate its on-demand content into commercial rival ProSiebenSat.1's streaming service Joyn from Q4 2026, free and without ads, resolving a dispute the courts decided in ZDF's favor last year. It joins a wave of similar moves: Channel 4 in talks with the BBC, TF1 onto Netflix, France Televisions onto Prime Video, and Disney+ content deals with four European broadcasters.
Why This Matters
The common structure across these pacts, carriage of existing catalogs with no joint commissioning, is the part that cuts against producers: distribution reach gets pooled while programming demand does not grow. Unlike a merger, no combined entity emerges with a larger commissioning budget, so the licensing buyer set per territory shrinks with nothing offsetting it. The State Media Treaty's encouragement of exactly this cooperation signals that European regulators will keep blessing consolidation in this form precisely because it stops short of formal combination.
The Facts

2026-07-20 Monday 5 stories

Judge Grants Temporary Restraining Order To Pause Paramount-Warner Bros Discovery Merger For 14 Days
The judge waived the bond requirement and set a preliminary-injunction hearing for August 3; Paramount owes WBD $7M for every day past September 30 the deal stays open.
CEO Summary
A federal judge granted the 12 state AGs a 14-day temporary restraining order barring Paramount and Warner Bros Discovery from closing or integrating their $110B merger, writing that the states' market-share evidence lets the court "presume the proposed merger is likely to violate antitrust laws." A preliminary-injunction hearing is set for August 3 and could extend the freeze indefinitely. Paramount owes WBD a $7M-per-day ticking fee for every day past September 30 the deal stays open.
Why This Matters
The ticking fee now cuts against its author: Paramount added the payment as a sweetener to win the bidding war for Warner Bros Discovery, and the litigation has converted it into the states' leverage. Every week of delay the states secure costs Paramount money under a term it volunteered and costs the states nothing, which means time favors the plaintiffs regardless of how the merits ultimately resolve.
Specific Implication
Counterparties in active deals with either company should treat the August 3 preliminary-injunction hearing, not the merger's original closing timeline, as the operative date for the litigation's effect on the companies' near-term posture.
The Facts
  • U.S. District Judge Araceli Martínez-Olguín granted a 14-day temporary restraining order, issued Monday (7/20), barring Paramount and Warner Bros Discovery from "closing or consummating the Transaction or taking any steps, directly or indirectly, to integrate or consolidate their operations."
  • The TRO responds to the antitrust suit filed by California AG Rob Bonta and 11 other state AGs, arguing the combined firm would hold outsized market share in wide-release theatrical distribution, top-grossing-film distribution, and basic-cable-channel licensing.
  • The judge wrote the states "present compelling evidence" of market-share concentration and that "on this combined firm market share alone, the Court is persuaded that it can presume the proposed merger is likely to violate antitrust laws."
  • States sought the TRO because Paramount had not guaranteed it wouldn't close after July 22 — the date the EU is expected to rule on the deal. At Friday's hearing, Paramount's lead attorney Jeffrey Kessler had already signaled a willingness to hold off closing for 28 days.
  • Judge waived Paramount/WBD's usual bond/security requirement given the "public's vital interest in antitrust enforcement."
  • Preliminary injunction schedule set: states' motion due Thursday, Paramount opposition due July 27, states' reply July 30, hearing August 3 — this could halt the merger indefinitely pending the outcome.
  • Financial stakes: Paramount faces a $7M-per-day "ticking fee" owed to Warner Bros for every day the deal isn't closed after September 30 — a sweetener Paramount added to win the WBD bidding war.
  • Paramount's spokesperson called the states' antitrust theory "without any basis in modern market realities" and vowed to "vigorously defend the transaction." Bonta: "This is a critical first win in our case to ensure this megamerger never sees the light of day." NY AG Letitia James echoed the consumer-harm framing.
  • Context: this had been trending toward a close — DOJ closed its own investigation in June and stated it did not view the deal as anticompetitive; the state-AG action is a separate, parallel track.
· Deadline (corroborated by Variety, THR, TheWrap, IndieWire) · Ted Johnson · 2026-07-20 · https://deadline.com/2026/07/paramount-warner-bros-lawsuit-restraining-order-1236998321/ · Provenance: filing/transcript source ↗
'The Odyssey' Opens to $124.5M Domestic, $264.1M Global: Universal's Biggest R-Rated Launch Ever
Premium large formats delivered 53% of the domestic gross and IMAX alone took $29.6M domestically; the film also broke the BFI IMAX opening-weekend record in the UK.
CEO Summary
"The Odyssey" opened to $124.5M domestic and $264.1M global, the biggest live-action opening of 2026 and Universal's highest R-rated launch ever. The $250M production drew an A CinemaScore and 95% from critics, the director's career best. Premium large formats delivered 53% of the domestic gross, and the film broke the BFI IMAX opening-weekend record in the UK.
Why This Matters
The audience skew is the finding with shelf life: half the opening-weekend audience was aged 18 to 34. The demographic the industry treats as lost to streaming paid the highest ticket prices in the market. That undercuts the assumption that premium-format event cinema skews old and identifies the format itself, rather than franchise familiarity, as what draws younger audiences to theaters.
Specific Implication
The film's premium-format share gives filmmakers pursuing an IMAX-native shoot on an original theatrical title a specific performance reference when negotiating for a gross definition that includes IMAX and PLF surcharges.

Here's Just How Top-Heavy Netflix Is Right Now
The top 20 shows, 0.24% of titles, drove 8.5% of Netflix's H1 series viewing, while the bottom 94% of shows combined managed 52.6% of series views.
CEO Summary
Netflix's semiannual viewership data for January through June 2026 shows the top 500 series drew 47% of all series views and the top 500 movies drew over 51% of movie views, out of 17,370 titles streamed. The top 20 shows alone, 0.24% of titles, drove 8.5% of series viewing. Netflix will move to annual reporting next year, reducing this visibility.
Why This Matters
The sharpest cut of the data sits at the very top: two shows and two movies, 0.02% of everything available, drove 2.1% of all viewing in the half. Viewing on the platform behaves like theatrical box office, a hit-concentrated business, while the platform's compensation norms were built on the premise that individual title performance is not what gets paid for. The concentration figures are the company's own evidence against its compensation model.
Specific Implication
Any deal with an engagement- or ranking-based bonus tied to Netflix's Top 10 or viewership rankings should be dated against this release. Netflix's shift to annual reporting means this may be the last semiannual snapshot published at this level of detail.
The Facts
  • Netflix's semiannual viewership data (Jan–June 2026) shows the top 500 series accounted for 47% of total series views, and the top 500 movies accounted for more than 51% of movie views — while representing roughly 6% of the 17,370 total titles streamed (8,193 shows, 9,177 movies) in the window.
  • Top 20 shows alone (0.24% of titles) drove 889.5M views — 8.5% of Netflix's H1 total series viewing.
  • The bottom ~94% of titles (7,693 of 8,193 shows) accounted for just 52.6% of total series views combined.
  • Top 2 shows + top 2 movies — just 0.02% of everything available — drove 2.1% of all Netflix views for the half.
  • Netflix is the only major platform that discloses this level of title-by-title data — and per THR, it will move to an annual report starting next year, reducing this visibility going forward.

AMC's own framing of the quarter, premium formats and cost containment, locates the record in yield per patron rather than in attendance alone. A Dozen States Sue to Block the Paramount–Warner Bros. Discovery Merger; Judge to Rule on TRO by July 22
The states built their case on 27% of wide-release theatrical share and 30% of anticipated blockbuster films; the ruling lands the same day as the EU's own approval deadline.
CEO Summary
Twelve state attorneys general, led by California's Rob Bonta, sued to block Paramount Skydance's $110B acquisition of Warner Bros. Discovery, a month after the DOJ cleared the deal. A federal judge heard the TRO motion Friday and will rule by July 22, the same day as the EU's provisional approval deadline. The suit alleges the combined company would control roughly 30% of anticipated top-grossing theatrical films.
Why This Matters
Paramount's defense, that the combined company is needed to compete with Netflix, answers a theatrical-concentration claim by pointing to a streamer, a move that treats theatrical and streaming as a single competitive market. The case therefore turns less on the share arithmetic than on whether the court accepts that market definition. If it does, the theatrical figures lose force. If it does not, Paramount is left defending concentration in exactly the markets the complaint defines, with its committed 30-plus films a year reading as a behavioral concession that the concern is real.
Specific Implication
Until the court rules and the acquisition closes, Paramount and Warner Bros. Discovery remain separate companies for contracting purposes. Agreements executed before a ruling or close are made with the entity that exists at signing, not with a post-merger combined company.
The Facts
A coalition of 12 state attorneys general (led by California's Rob Bonta) filed an antitrust suit on 2026-07-13 in the US District Court for the Northern District of California seeking to block Paramount Skydance's $110B acquisition of Warner Bros. Discovery — a month after the DOJ cleared the deal. The suit alleges Clayton Act violations across three markets: wide-release theatrical distribution, "anticipated top-grossing" theatrical distribution, and basic-cable-channel licensing to distributors — a combined Paramount/WBD would hold ~27% share of wide-release theatrical distribution and control ~30% of anticipated blockbuster films. On Friday 2026-07-17, a federal judge heard 80 minutes of argument on the states' TRO motion and said she'd rule by July 22 — the date Paramount's counsel says the deal will not close by regardless. The EU has its own provisional approval deadline of July 22; the UK CMA has an open investigation. Paramount calls the suit "wrong on both the facts and the law" and argues the combined company is needed to compete with Netflix; Paramount's CEO has committed to 30+ films/year post-close.

CEO Summary
A24 is offering "Backrooms" director Kane Parsons, 21, a three-year first-look deal estimated around $65M, its largest offer ever to an individual director, to keep him from Warner Bros., HBO, Universal, and Amazon MGM, all actively courting him. Parsons' $10M film grossed $374M globally, A24's biggest release ever. Universal separately kept "Obsession" director Curry Barker in the fold with a rich eight-figure deal for his third film.
Why This Matters
The structure of the A24 offer is the tell. The headline value rests on performance bonuses tied to two films that do not yet exist, which makes the reported number largely contingent rather than guaranteed compensation. The bidding war is therefore inflating quoted deal sizes faster than committed money, and the studios are buying option value on an audience pipeline rather than paying present-day rates for a proven filmography.
Specific Implication
Representatives of early-career genre filmmakers with a breakout result can cite these offers as comps for multi-picture, first-look money before a second film exists.
The Facts
"Backrooms" director Kane Parsons (21, whose $10M film is A24's top-grossing release ever at $374M global) is being courted by multiple studios for his next projects. Warner Bros. film chiefs flew to meet him in person to pitch an overall deal spanning film and TV; he then took a meeting with HBO leadership, since the Warners pitch bundles film and TV. Universal's chief has also met with him, fresh off winning "Obsession" director Curry Barker for his next film. A24 is countering with a three-year first-look deal including performance bonuses tied to two films; one source estimates the payday around $65M, still being negotiated — A24's largest offer ever to an individual director. Separately, Barker's "Obsession" follow-up is already shot at a Universal specialty label; mid-"Obsession"'s $400M run, a rival studio offered him $10M sight-unseen for his next original, but he stayed within the Universal family for his third film in what was described as a rich eight-figure deal. Elsewhere this cycle: Warner Bros. won a five-studio bidding war for the underlying rights to a viral horror-meme property, and Amazon MGM won a separate bidding war for the film rights to a YouTube-released horror franchise.

CEO Summary
