The $250M Universal release beat the studio's own $80-100M domestic projection by more than 25%, with an A CinemaScore and 95% on Rotten Tomatoes. It is also the year's top R-rated opening and Universal's best R-rated opening ever.
Why This Matters
The $125M global marketing spend is the detail that exposes the mispricing: Universal supported the film at full franchise scale while forecasting it well below what it delivered. The studio spent like it believed in the title and projected like it was hedging. The gap between those two behaviors is a measure of how much internal models still discount originals relative to what the market will pay for them.
Specific Implication
The result sets a recent comp for greenlighting an original theatrical release above $200M on the strength of a name director and ensemble cast, without pre-existing IP attached.

The $250M production (shot entirely on IMAX cameras, plus $125M in global marketing) earned an A CinemaScore and 95% Rotten Tomatoes, exceeding Universal's own pre-release projection of $80-100M domestic by 25%+. Universal's president of domestic theatrical distribution, Jim Orr, called it proof that "Christopher Nolan continues to create films that are immersive theatrical events."

Midjourney Escalates Bid to Force Disney, Universal & Warner Bros. to Reveal Their Own Internal AI Use
A magistrate judge had limited discovery to consumer-facing AI tools; Midjourney's renewed motion seeks the studios' internal training data, model weights, board presentations and employee prompts.
CEO Summary
Midjourney renewed its motion to force Disney, Universal, and Warner Bros., the studios suing it for copyright infringement, to turn over records of their own internal AI use, including training datasets, model weights, board presentations, and every prompt studio employees have entered into Midjourney itself. A magistrate judge had limited discovery to consumer-facing AI applications. The studios call the motion a "fishing expedition."
Why This Matters
Midjourney's argument, that the studios are withholding documents showing whether they use unlicensed-trained AI internally, converts the infringement claim into a symmetry test. If discovery runs both directions, the plaintiffs' own internal AI practices become part of the price of pressing the case. That raises the settlement value of the dispute independent of the merits and explains why the fight over discovery scope has become the case's center of gravity.
Specific Implication
If the motion succeeds, the studios' internal AI practices would enter the public docket in that litigation. That disclosure would be directly relevant to any counterparty negotiating AI provenance or consent language with Disney, Universal, or Warner Bros.
The Facts
Disney and Universal sued Midjourney for copyright infringement over its models' ability to generate copyrighted characters; Warner Bros. filed a consolidated suit months later. A magistrate judge ruled in June that the studios only have to produce discovery on "consumer-facing" AI applications. Midjourney's July filing sought to overturn that limit, arguing the studios are withholding documents that would show whether they use unlicensed-trained AI internally — the same conduct they're suing Midjourney over. Per later reporting, Midjourney renewed the motion the week of 2026-07-13, now seeking internal AI business plans, training datasets, model weights, board-level presentations, and every prompt studio employees have entered into Midjourney itself. The studios' lead attorney calls it a "fishing expedition." The matter is in active discovery.

DGA, WGA & SAG-AFTRA Jointly Protest Emmys' Decision to Cut Five Categories From the Main NBC Telecast
The guilds can withhold residual-fee waivers normally granted for main-telecast clip usage, the same leverage that forced the Academy to reverse an identical cut in 2023.
CEO Summary
The Television Academy voted to move five categories off the main NBC Emmy telecast on Sept. 14 to the earlier Creative Arts ceremonies, cutting the show from 24 to 19 televised awards. The cuts hit limited-series supporting acting, writing, and directing, plus variety-series writing. The DGA, WGA, and SAG-AFTRA issued a joint statement protesting the move.
Why This Matters
The Academy justified the cuts by the telecast's fixed three-hour runtime, which frames broadcast minutes as the scarce asset being reallocated. The guilds' waiver mechanism effectively prices those minutes, so the dispute functions as a rate negotiation over airtime between the Academy and the crafts rather than a symbolic fight over prestige. It will resolve on whichever side's economics give first.
Specific Implication
The residual-waiver leverage described in the reporting, and its 2023 precedent, indicate the category cuts are not necessarily final pending further guild action.
The Facts
The Television Academy's Board of Governors voted to move five categories off the main primetime Emmy broadcast (NBC, Sept. 14) to the earlier Creative Arts Emmys (Sept. 5-6): Supporting Actor and Supporting Actress in a Limited or Anthology Series or Movie, Writing and Directing for a Limited or Anthology Series or Movie, and Writing for a Variety Series. The main show drops from 24 to 19 televised categories; the Academy cites limited three-hour runtime. The DGA, WGA, and SAG-AFTRA issued a joint statement calling the move a decision that "devalues the contributions of the talented people" the ceremony exists to honor. The guilds retain real leverage: they can decline to waive residual fees normally waived for main-telecast clip usage, contingent on a minimum category count per craft — the same lever that forced a public reversal in 2023 when a similar WGA protest succeeded.

The Paramount-WBD Fight Comes Down To One Question: What Counts As A Blockbuster
Paramount cited Obsession, made for $750,000 and grossing near $430 million, as proof the market is open; a ticking fee of roughly $650 million per quarter starts Oct. 1 if unclosed.
CEO Summary
Judge Araceli Martínez-Olguín heard the state AGs' emergency motion against Paramount's $110B acquisition of Warner Bros. Discovery on Friday and will rule in writing before July 22, the date of an anticipated EU greenlight and the earliest possible close. The hearing turned on what counts as a blockbuster: Paramount cited the $750K creator film Obsession grossing $430M as proof the market is open; the states answered that five majors make blockbusters consistently. A ticking fee of roughly $650M per quarter starts Oct. 1.
Why This Matters
The most revealing detail is the rejected remedy. Paramount pushed to settle pre-filing with behavioral commitments, including 30 movies a year at a 45-day theatrical window, and the states refused anything short of structural separation. That refusal defines the endgame. The states are not negotiating for output guarantees, so no windowing or volume concession Paramount can offer resolves the case. It can only end in a divestiture, a court loss for the states, or Paramount absorbing the delay cost until one of those arrives.
Specific Implication
A counterparty negotiating output, windowing, or distribution terms with Paramount or Warner Bros. Discovery should treat the ruling due before July 22 as the operative date. That ruling, not either party's own stated timeline, determines whether either company's negotiating position or deal capacity changes.
The Facts
ProceedingFriday hearing before U.S. District Judge Araceli Martínez-Olguín (N.D. Cal., Oakland) on the state AGs' emergency motion for a TRO / preliminary injunction against Paramount's acquisition of Warner Bros. Discovery
Ruling timingJudge will issue a written ruling before July 22 — the date of an anticipated EU antitrust greenlight and the earliest the merger could theoretically close
Deal size$110–111B (sources vary); 12 states led by California AG Rob Bonta
Markets allegedThree: blockbuster films, wide-release movies, and cable networks
Ticking feeThe controlling shareholders agreed to pay 25 cents cash per share per quarter — roughly $650M per quarter, cited as ~$7.2M/day (Deadline) and ~$6.9M/day (THR) — to WBD shareholders for each day the deal is unclosed starting Oct. 1
Paramount's offerCounsel offered to forgo closing early if the court rules on a preliminary injunction by early September; the AGs asked for proceedings next year
States' share claimCombined company would take more than a quarter of every box office dollar; ~30% share of blockbuster distribution
Rejected remedyParamount pushed to settle pre-filing with behavioral commitments including 30 movies a year at a 45-day theatrical window; the states refused, wanting structural relief (separating a film studio, cable channels, or a news channel)

The hearing's central exchange was definitional. Paramount's counsel cited Obsession — made for $750,000 by a digital creator and grossing close to $430M worldwide via Focus Features — as "undisputed economic evidence" that the majors no longer control blockbuster supply. Counsel for the states answered that breakouts do not define a market: "I'm not saying no one can have a super hit or a breakout, but there are five majors that make blockbusters consistently."

Paramount also pointed to streamers entering theatrical, citing F1 as an Amazon success before correcting that it was an Apple film. The states' retort: Apple made "a successful big movie, but it was distributed by Warner Bros. Apple makes cell phones, not movies."

Divergence flagged: earlier reporting named a different district judge as assigned to the emergency motion; today's Deadline and THR accounts both place Judge Araceli Martínez-Olguín on the bench for Friday's hearing. This file records Martínez-Olguín per the two same-day accounts, with the earlier attribution unreconciled.

An early-stage deal in any of the named experiment areas is a test the company has effectively pre-announced its willingness to abandon before investing at material scale.
Specific Implication
An agreement with a Netflix viewership-based bonus, renewal, or performance trigger should specify a defined reporting metric, audit right, and disclosure schedule in the contract rather than relying on Netflix's voluntary reporting. That reporting has moved from twice-yearly to annual. Separately, TF1's channels being added to the service through a bundling arrangement is described by Netflix as a distribution partnership rather than a content commission. That distinction is relevant to how comparable arrangements are classified in future deals.
The Facts
Free trialsNetflix is reintroducing free trials in some markets, a practice it abandoned in 2020; not currently available in the U.S. (first reported by What's On Netflix)
Data cadenceAlongside Q2 earnings, Netflix said it will move from twice-yearly show-level viewership reporting to once annually; granular reporting only began in Dec. 2023
Active experimentsYouTube-style shows, video podcasts, live-streamed broadcast channels, cloud-based video games
BundlingFrance's TF1 channels are being bundled into Netflix, with more partnerships signalled
Sarandos on method"When we expand into new entertainment offerings, new initiatives, we do it gradually. We do it where we believe we can add more value for our members, and we do it where we believe we have the right to win. And then we look for the positive signals before we invest at material scale."
Peters on partnerships"Whether through licensing or through new partnerships like TF1, we believe that we can help other producers, other services maximize the value, the relevance of the content that they invest in by finding those bigger audiences."

THR's framing is that Netflix has historically reversed itself on ads, live sports and originals, and that the current cluster of moves reads as a deliberate return to disruptor behavior: "Now it needs to disrupt itself."

Three Guilds Jointly Protest The Emmys Cutting Five Craft Categories, With Residual Fees As The Real Lever
A 2023 attempt to cut Writing for a Variety Series drew the same guild pushback and was reversed for 2024, and the Academy must negotiate a new TV agreement after this year's ceremony.
CEO Summary
DGA, WGA and SAG-AFTRA jointly protested the TV Academy's decision to move five categories off the main 2026 Emmy telecast, including supporting acting, writing and directing for limited or anthology series. The guilds waive hundreds of thousands of dollars in clip residuals contingent on the broadcast carrying a minimum number of categories per field, and the Academy must negotiate a new TV agreement after this year's ceremony.
Why This Matters
The Academy's stated rationale, replacing lower-commercial-appeal categories with content expected to draw viewers in pursuit of a larger license fee, prices the telecast in ratings terms. Its cost base, though, is denominated in a currency the guilds control. The ratings bet only pays if the residual subsidy survives it, so the economics of the broadcast depend on guild consent rather than on the category lineup itself.
Specific Implication
Given the 2023 precedent and the guilds' residual-waiver leverage, the category lineup for this year's telecast should be treated as unsettled. That applies to anyone planning an awards campaign around a title in one of the five moved categories.

A Major Streamer Puts $587M On the Record for In-House Post-Production AI
The startup was a three-year-old, 16-person stealth team whose movie-star-producer founder has joined Netflix as a senior adviser; the company says roughly 300 titles used generative AI this year.
CEO Summary
Netflix's 10-Q discloses a March acquisition of a 16-person stealth AI startup for approximately $587M in cash. The company builds models from a production's own dailies to accelerate post-production, and its movie-star-producer founder has joined Netflix as a senior adviser. On the same week's earnings call, the co-CEO said roughly 300 titles used generative AI this year.
Why This Matters
The structure is the story. Buying the company outright rather than licensing its tools converts post-production savings from a vendor cost into an owned asset. The efficiencies extracted from each production's dailies accrue to the streamer's margin rather than to the production's budget. That is vertical integration of a cost center. It puts the buyer on the opposite side of the table from its own productions on the question of who benefits when their footage improves the model.
Specific Implication
Representatives negotiating post-production or overall deals with the streamer should treat AI-training consent and data-use terms as provisions to specify rather than assume are covered by existing dailies-access language.
The Facts
The disclosureNetflix's Form 10-Q (filed Friday) states it completed an acquisition in March "for a total purchase price of approximately $587 million" in cash
What was boughtA three-year-old, stealth-mode AI startup — a 16-person team of engineers, researchers and creatives — founded and led by a movie-star-producer who has joined the streamer as a senior adviser
The toolingBuilds an AI model from a production's own dailies to accelerate post-production (mixing, relighting shots, adding VFX) — explicitly not typical generative image-making; framed as keeping "filmmakers at the center"
Earnings-call contextOn Thursday's Q2 call, the co-CEO said roughly 300 of the streamer's titles used some form of generative AI this year, citing one docuseries produced "twice as fast and at half the cost"
The framing"We believe it takes great artists to make something great, and AI is not changing that… AI provides them with better tools."

Paramount-WBD: Emergency Motions, A Showdown Hearing And Next Steps
The states' theory is modeled on DOJ's Penguin Random House win over author pay; Paramount counters with AT&T-Time Warner, a case the government lost that was unwound three years later.
CEO Summary
Twelve state AGs led by California's Rob Bonta filed for a TRO and preliminary injunction against Paramount's $110B acquisition of Warner Bros. Discovery, with Judge P. Casey Pitts asked to rule before July 22 and a hearing set for Friday. The states' theory rests on a narrow "anticipated top-grossing films" submarket, modeled on DOJ's successful Penguin Random House challenge. The WGA filed a separate labor-market suit the same day.
Why This Matters
The precedent choice defines the case. By importing the Penguin Random House framework, where the injury was to what authors were paid, the states have filed what is functionally a creator-compensation case wearing a distribution-share complaint. If that framing survives the emergency stage, studio consolidation gets measured in court against what sellers of films and labor are paid, a theory with reach well beyond this merger.
Specific Implication
The merger's outcome will not be resolved by the July 22 emergency ruling or the Friday hearing, and the litigation could extend well past that date. Deals under negotiation with either company should therefore carry a change-of-control clause rather than assume the current corporate structure holds for the length of a long-term commitment.
The Facts
ActionCalifornia AG Rob Bonta + 11 other state AGs filed for emergency relief (TRO + preliminary injunction) against Paramount's $110B acquisition of Warner Bros. Discovery
JudgeU.S. District Judge P. Casey Pitts, assigned Tuesday; asked to rule on the emergency motion before July 22; hearing set for Friday
States' market-share claim~27% of wide-release theatrical distribution (600+ theatre threshold); ~30% of "anticipated top-grossing" distribution (3,000+ theatre threshold); Paramount-WBD + Disney together = 59% of top-grossing films; 27% of basic cable by affiliate fees, 34% by viewership
RelatedWGA filed a separate challenge today focused on labor-market harm; a consumer-lawsuit preliminary injunction was denied separately
DefenseParamount calls the suit a "flawed application of antitrust laws"; Jeffrey Kessler (CNBC): "they would take this up to the Supreme Court if they had to"

Paramount's core argument is that the merger is pro-competitive because a stronger rival is needed against Netflix. The states' theory rests on a narrow submarket — "anticipated top-grossing films" — explicitly modeled on DOJ's successful Penguin Random House/Simon & Schuster challenge, where the market was "anticipated top-selling books" and the harm was to author payments.

The Box Office Bounces Back: Gen Z, Surprise Hits And More Have Hollywood Banking On A $10 Billion Year
Obsession grossed more than $426 million on a $750,000 budget, with its second, third and fourth weekends each outgrossing its opening frame.
CEO Summary
Hollywood is tracking toward a $10B domestic year on an unusually diverse hit list: a $1B Nintendo adaptation, the first $1B biopic, a $688M comedy sequel, a $683M original for Amazon MGM, a $426M horror film made for $750K, and a $363M A24 debut from a YouTube-native creator. Exhibition and studio voices credit slate balance and Gen Z turnout.
Why This Matters
No two of the year's hits come from the same playbook, and that composition is the argument. Kustermann's "diverse balance within the slate" names the variable that changed, and it cuts against the concentration logic studios adopted after COVID. If six different bet types can pay in the same year, the scarce input is not pre-awareness but a calendar that spreads risk across categories, which removes the standard argument for passing on originals.
Specific Implication
On a return profile like Obsession's, backend terms such as first-dollar gross or a low breakeven threshold become a larger share of total compensation than the production budget itself. That structure is worth specifying on any low-budget original with word-of-mouth potential.

'M3GAN' Spinoff 'Soulm8te' Goes Straight To Digital After Theatrical Release Pulled
The M3GAN franchise went from $181 million worldwide to $39 million to no theatrical release at all across three films in three years; Soulm8te rents digitally starting Aug. 1.
CEO Summary
Soulm8te, the M3GAN spinoff pulled from Universal's theatrical calendar late last year, will go straight to digital rental on Aug 1. The franchise's trajectory across three films in three years: $181M worldwide, then $39M, then no theatrical release at all.
Why This Matters
The disposition was decided by a different film's numbers: the sequel's $10 million domestic opening is the data point that killed this title's theatrical run, months before any audience could evaluate the spinoff itself. That is franchise-level underwriting applied to an individual film, and it means a project sold as part of a horror franchise now inherits the trajectory of the previous installment's opening weekend.
Specific Implication
A theatrical release date listed on a contract or calendar is not a guaranteed commitment, as this title's removal demonstrates. Deal terms that assume a theatrical window should include either an enforceable release commitment or a payment trigger that activates if the release is pulled or downgraded.
The Facts

Marvel Comics To Leave New York And Move Staffers To L.A. As Part Of Major Overhaul
The relocation's practical trigger is a Manhattan lease on Avenue of the Americas expiring next year, and an internal audit found more U.S.-based creators now live in California than New York.
CEO Summary
Marvel Comics leaves New York after roughly 90 years, relocating about 100 publishing employees to Burbank by July 2027, next to Disney and the film operation. Stephen Wacker replaces C.B. Cebulski as editor-in-chief; Cebulski moves to Japan to lead a manga and Asia-originals push. The division recently lost its comics market-share lead for the first time this century.
Why This Matters
The response reveals how the parent scores the division. A market-share loss in publishing was answered not with a publishing strategy but with physical integration into the screen operation. That treats comics as upstream development for film and television rather than a business to be fixed on its own terms. With the DC precedent from 2015, both major American comic publishers now sit inside studio complexes, so the format's commissioning decisions will increasingly be filtered through adaptation potential.
Specific Implication
The relocation requires roughly 100 New York-based employees to move cross-country within about a year, and some editorial staff may not make the move. Whether the talent responsible for the division's output relocates, rather than the office move itself, will determine whether the reorganization preserves the value of Marvel's comics source material.
The Facts

Paramount-WBD: Emergency Motions, A Showdown Hearing And Next Steps In Challenge To Mega-Merger
Paramount's counsel, Jeff Kessler, told CNBC the company would take the case up to the Supreme Court if it had to; the DOJ closed its own investigation in June with no objection.
CEO Summary
California AG Rob Bonta and 11 other state AGs filed for a TRO and preliminary injunction to halt Paramount's roughly $110B acquisition of Warner Bros. Discovery, with a hearing set for Friday and a ruling requested before July 22. The states allege the combined company would hold about 30% of the "anticipated top-grossing" theatrical market and, together with Disney, 59% of top-grossing film distribution. The DOJ already closed its own investigation in June with no objection.
Why This Matters
The complaint's leverage lives in its market definitions rather than its concentration math. The alleged theatrical share moves from about 27% to about 30% depending on whether the screen threshold is 600 or 3,000 theatres. A challenge whose headline number shifts with the screen-count cutoff invites the court to dispose of the case on definitional grounds without ever reaching the merits of consolidation. Friday's hearing is therefore less a referendum on the merger than on whether the states' narrowest market survives first contact.
Specific Implication
Any deal or negotiation premised on Warner Bros. Discovery's status as an independent company, or on Paramount's post-merger scale, should treat that status as unresolved until the July 22 ruling.
The Facts
ItemDetail
ActionCalifornia AG Rob Bonta + 11 other state AGs filed for emergency relief — TRO + preliminary injunction — to halt Paramount's acquisition of Warner Bros. Discovery
Deal size~$110B (THR reports $111B)
JudgeU.S. District Judge P. Casey Pitts (case reassigned Tuesday); asked to rule on the emergency motion before July 22; hearing set Friday
Alleged marketsWide-release theatrical distribution (600+ theatre threshold): ~27% · anticipated top-grossing theatrical (3,000+ theatre threshold): ~30% · basic cable by affiliate fees: 27% · by viewership: 34%
Concentration claimParamount-WBD + Disney together = 59% of the top-grossing film distribution market
Companion suitsWGA filed a separate challenge (labor-market focus); a Paramount investor sued David and Larry Ellison over an alleged Trump/CNN side deal

Prior federal clearance is already in hand: DOJ closed its investigation on June 12 with no objection. The WSJ reported that career antitrust staffers were leaning toward a challenge before senior leadership closed the probe; AG-nominee Todd Blanche told Sen. Cory Booker on July 15 "I was part of that decision" but had "no idea" what career attorneys recommended.

Paramount Calls AGs' Case "One Of The Weakest Merger Challenges In Modern Antitrust History" As Consumer Injunction Is Denied
Paramount's filing names A24, Neon, Universal, Disney, Amazon MGM, Sony and Lionsgate as rivals with low entry barriers, and calls its cable lineup and WBD's complements, not substitutes.
CEO Summary
Paramount filed its opposition to the state AGs' TRO motion, calling it "one of the weakest merger challenges in modern antitrust history" and the $110B WBD merger procompetitive. The same day, Judge Araceli Martínez-Olguín, newly assigned to the case, denied a preliminary injunction sought by five consumer plaintiffs for lack of any evidence. The AGs' hearing is Friday; the deal still plans to close in Q3.
Why This Matters
Paramount's written representation that the merger will increase the output of theatrical releases, filed among its claimed benefits, is the concession worth banking. Once a merging party asserts a procompetitive benefit in a court filing, state negotiators can demand it back as an enforceable condition in any settlement. That converts an advocacy point into an output floor the combined studio would have to live with.
The Facts

Netflix Shares Fall After Mixed Q2; Company Concedes "Not All Hours Are Equal"
Revenue came in at $12.56 billion, a slight miss, while EPS beat by a penny at 80 cents; full-year guidance narrowed to $51 billion to $51.4 billion.
CEO Summary
Netflix reported Q2 revenue of $12.56B, a slight miss, and EPS of 80 cents, a penny beat; shares fell 9% after hours to an 18-month low. First-half viewing grew just 2% to 97 billion hours, and the shareholder letter declared "not all hours are equal." Full-year guidance narrowed to $51B-$51.4B.
Why This Matters
The shareholder letter's pivot to "quality and variety" retires the one engagement measure outsiders could audit without naming a successor, leaving the company as sole arbiter of whether a title performed. Until a defined replacement metric exists, that asymmetry favors the buyer in every renewal, bonus and participation conversation keyed to performance.
Specific Implication
Any deal term keyed to Netflix engagement or viewership performance should specify the exact metric and its definition in writing. The company has stated that hours watched no longer fully determines how it evaluates a title's performance.

Netflix Cuts Viewership Disclosure From Twice A Year To Once, Effective Q1 2027
Returning series fell from 7 of the top 10 titles to 3, and Bridgerton season 4 is the first season of that franchise to miss Netflix's all-time list.
CEO Summary
Netflix will cut its What We Watched viewership report from twice a year to once, published each Q1 starting in 2027. The change lands in the same disclosure as a 2% engagement increase and amid reporting on steep second-season dropoffs, which Sarandos denied are material. Netflix remains the only major streamer publishing regular viewing data at all.
Why This Matters
Sarandos's defense rests on "bands of expectation" that exist only inside the company, which converts the season-two dispute into a claim no outside party can test. The disclosure change compounds the asymmetry. The next comprehensive public data point on whether returning series are decaying will not arrive until early 2027, after another full cycle of renewal decisions has been made on numbers only Netflix holds.
Specific Implication
Any bonus, escalator, or renewal trigger tied to Netflix's published viewership data should specify delivery timing and audit rights in the contract. The report that would inform such a trigger will now arrive on an annual, not semiannual, cycle.
The Facts
ItemDetail

Last biannual editionToday's H1 2026 report is the final twice-yearly one
HistoryBiannual reports ran four years; only attached to the earnings cycle starting last year
Stated reason (shareholder memo)"Engagement is not just the quantity of view hours, but also refers to the quality and variety of our offering. To make this clearer, we're making a change to our view hours disclosure."
Second stated reason"The goal of separating the publication of the report from our earnings results is to keep the focus on our primary financial metrics – revenue and operating profit."

Netflix Content Spend Rises To ~$20B As Sarandos Says Gen AI Has Been Used On 300 Titles
The cited example is 17 minutes of AI-enhanced footage in a documentary, produced twice as fast at half the cost; live programming will be about 5 percent of total spend.
CEO Summary
Netflix will spend about $20B on content in 2026, up roughly 10%, and Ted Sarandos said generative AI workflows have been used on about 300 titles, concentrated in post-production. He cited a documentary sequence produced twice as fast at half the cost and said the savings are reinvested into more content.
Why This Matters
Every disclosed use sits on the post-production side of the line that guild AI provisions police most closely, principal photography and performance. Sarandos's remark that these use cases "are scaling faster and faster" is the operative signal. Post-production is where AI savings compound without triggering the consent and notice obligations that attach once the tools touch cast or set. Adoption will therefore grow fastest exactly where contractual visibility is weakest.
Specific Implication
Any budget or savings figure referencing "twice as fast, at half the cost" should be checked against the underlying production type. Netflix's cited example was a documentary post-production sequence rather than a scripted production with cast and crew.
The Facts
ItemDetail
2026 content spend~$20B, up ~10% — accelerating from ~8% annual increases over the last five years, but below the 14% decade average
Live programmingWill be about 5% of total content spend
Gen AI penetrationGen AI workflows used on roughly 300 Netflix titles, concentrated in post-production
Named use cases"Really complicated shots and sequences… enhancing crowds, or historical battle scenes"
Worked exampleDocumentary series The American Experiment — 17 minutes of AI-enhanced footage, produced twice as fast at half the cost of previous options
Sarandos on accessProductions "would have left out those key shots because they just wouldn't have been able to afford them. So they're saved by availability and access to these Gen AI tools."
ScalingAI use cases "are scaling faster and faster"
Where savings goReinvested into more content, feeding the "whole revenue, profit flywheel"

The Batman Part II's new Feb. 18, 2028 slot is already occupied by an untitled Disney movie and Sony's Grandgear; prior Presidents Day openers there include Black Panther.
CEO Summary
Warner Bros. moved four release dates in one motion: The Great Beyond to Oct 1, 2027, The Batman Part II to Feb 18, 2028, and a swap of Panic Carefully and Revenge of La Llorona across early 2027. The Batman Part II has now slipped from a fall 2027 launchpad to a February holiday weekend, roughly six years after the original. Two of the new dates land directly against films already dated by Paramount, Warner's prospective acquirer.
Why This Matters
A studio does not ordinarily date two films in one motion directly against a single rival's dated titles, and doing it against the prospective acquirer is the tell. Parking Panic Carefully opposite Boys for Life and Revenge of La Llorona opposite K-Pop: The Debut makes sense only if Warner Bros. expects those conflicts to be resolved on a combined calendar after close. The date grid is behaving as a merger-confidence signal while the courts still treat the outcome as open.
Specific Implication
Any release-date-linked bonus or escalator tied to The Batman Part II's prior fall 2027 date should be reviewed. That date no longer applies, and the trigger language may not survive the new February 2028 window.

Netflix Co-CEOs On M&A: "Primarily Builders, Not Buyers" — And No Near-Term FAST Tier
Netflix collected a $2.8 billion breakup fee after its WBD bid failed, and its stock did not rebound afterward; the company points to its 330 million households as distribution scale.
CEO Summary
Asked directly about Lionsgate and NBCUniversal, Netflix's co-CEOs said the company remains "primarily builders, not buyers" with a "very high bar" for big M&A, the same language it used before bidding for WBD. Peters said there are no near-term plans for a free ad-supported tier, and the TF1 live-broadcast partnership in France was framed as a model the company would repeat.
Why This Matters
The load-bearing statement is not the M&A denial but the footprint framing. Pitching 330 million households as a way to help "other producers, other services maximize the value" of their content describes acquiring the economics of other companies' libraries through distribution terms. No purchase price and no merger review are involved. Repeated across enough partners, that structure concentrates the same market position an acquisition would, outside the reach of the antitrust process currently testing the rest of the industry.
Specific Implication
Netflix's characterization of the TF1 arrangement as a distribution partnership rather than an acquisition points to a deal shape: distributing existing IP through Netflix's reach without a sale. A rights holder wary of an outright sale could raise that as a comparable structure.

Creatives from 'Sharknado' and 'Doctor Who' Are Now Making AI Films
Promise AI is backed by North Road, Google and a16z, and the animated short series Hardcore 94, pairing writer Ivan Rome with AI artist Metapuppet, is already in production.
CEO Summary
Promise AI, an LA startup backed by North Road, Google and a16z, has paired credited working professionals, including a Doctor Who director and the writer who developed Sharknado, with AI filmmakers on named projects, with one animated short series already in production. All financing so far comes from Promise; mainstream distribution remains unsecured.
Why This Matters
The threshold crossed is reputational rather than technical: credited professionals paid to attach their names are what erode a norm the trade itself describes as historically taboo. Once refusal to work on AI films stops being the default position of working writers, holding out becomes an individually priced choice rather than a standard. That weakens the collective posture guild negotiators depend on before any contract language changes.
Specific Implication
Promise AI's Pixar comparison, iterating development drafts faster and cheaper, locates the emerging AI cost pressure at the development and rewrite stage rather than on-set production.

On the record. Mitchell: "People who work on AI films have a mastery that very few people have — you need to learn dozens of tools. And a [traditional] writer can bring in all their talent." On the Pixar analogy: "Look at what Pixar does, constantly putting things up on a feed and rewriting them over and over again. But that's really expensive. AI can do it much faster and cheaper — and not just for animation." Christopher Nolan's 'The Odyssey' To Begin Epic Journey With Around $200M WW – Box Office Preview
Four European Imax 70mm sites sold out a year in advance, while Moana, opening the same week, managed just a $47.5 million four-day domestic start.
CEO Summary
Universal projects Christopher Nolan's The Odyssey to open at $85M-$100M domestic plus about $110M overseas, a $200M+ global start, with $30M-$40M already in advance sales and no other major studio wide release on the weekend. The benchmark is Oppenheimer: an $82.4M domestic bow that legged to $330M, a 4x multiple.
Why This Matters
The projection rests on legs rather than measured demand. Advance sales trail the recent event-release benchmarks in the same data, Wicked: For Good above $60 million and Deadpool & Wolverine at $50 million, yet the forecast still assumes an event-scale start. Universal is pricing the opening off Nolan's multiplier history, so the number that settles the bet is the second-weekend hold. That hold is the first test of whether a three-hour R-rated original behaves like an event film once reservation-driven demand is spent.

The Video Game IP Boom Is About to Hit the Wall
Borderlands cost more than $110 million and returned just $33 million globally, and Xbox laid off 3,200 workers, a fifth of its staff, after its spending strategy misfired.
CEO Summary
More than half of the 50 best-selling game franchises of all time are already adapted or in development, and the dominant younger-audience titles, Fortnite, Minecraft and Roblox at roughly 40% of playtime, have almost no story to adapt. Gaming's own economics, 7-to-10-year builds, $1B+ budgets and mass layoffs, are not generating replacement franchises.
Why This Matters
The remaining pool is thinner than the halfway count implies: of the unadapted top-50 franchises, three originated in other media and six are sports games, leaving little that is both narrative and available. With supply compressed that far while buyers from Netflix to A24 to Paramount stay active in the category, the predictable failure mode is late-cycle pricing on mid-tier titles that lack the built-in audiences the early adaptations traded on. That is where the next Borderlands-scale loss gets made.
Specific Implication
Rights holders of the remaining unadapted narrative game franchises are negotiating scarce supply against buyers still active in the category. The Borderlands loss is the comp buyers will cite to hold price on mid-tier titles.
The Facts
ItemDetail
Supply ceilingMore than half of the 50 best-selling gaming franchises of all time are already adapted or in development. Of the remainder: 3 are reverse cases (Star Wars, The Walking Dead, Marvel's Spider-Man), 6 are sports games
"Black hole games"Fortnite, Minecraft, Roblox account for 40% of PC playtime and 43% of console playtime (Matthew Ball, State of Video Gaming, 2025)
Downside compBorderlands (2024): $110M+ budget → $33M global
Upside compsThe Super Mario Bros. Movie (2023, animated); A Minecraft Movie (2025)
Industry stressXbox laid off 3,200 — a fifth of its workforce — after its spending/streaming strategy misfired
AAA cost curve~$5M mid-'90s → "hundreds of millions" by 2019 (Layden, ex-PlayStation); GTA VI budget estimated $1B+
Dev timelines7–10 years for a AAA title
Hardware barrierNew PlayStation/Xbox $600–$900; Nintendo Switch 2 $450 — pushing young players to mobile, existing PCs, older consoles

Marvel Comics Leaves New York For Burbank After 90 Years, With A New Editor-In-Chief
The move was forced by a Manhattan lease expiring next year, and Marvel has lost its comics market-share lead for the first time this century.
CEO Summary
Marvel's publishing division will leave Manhattan after 90 years, relocating just over 100 employees to Burbank by July 2027, with Stephen Wacker becoming editor-in-chief and outgoing EIC C.B. Cebulski moving to Japan to lead a manga push. Publishing now reports into Brad Winderbaum, head of television, animation, comics and franchise.
Why This Matters
The tell is in who presided: the town hall announcing publishing's future was run by Winderbaum and Abdo, studio-side executives, not by anyone from publishing. That converts the comics line from an independent originator into a development arm whose commissioning choices will be steered by what the screen organization can use. That change in what gets made operates before any individual adaptation decision is taken.
Specific Implication
Cebulski's move to lead Marvel's manga push, building on the existing Shonen Jump partnership, establishes a named, active counterparty at Marvel for any IP or format crossover into manga.

Netflix Heads Into Q2 Earnings With Something To Prove: Does Wall Street See Another Revival?
Bernstein's Laurent Yoon flags possible cuts to 2026 margin guidance, and Netflix is reportedly eyeing a Letterboxd acquisition after its Warner Bros. merger agreement ended.
CEO Summary
Netflix reported Q2 results Thursday after the close with its stock at an 18-month low, down 40% over the past year. Consensus expected $12.58B in revenue and $0.79 EPS, both near the company's own projections. The bear case has shifted from subscriber growth to engagement, specifically reporting that many series show steep viewership dropoffs between seasons one and two.
Why This Matters
The quietest fact carries the most information: Netflix declined to raise full-year guidance in April, before the engagement reporting surfaced, which suggests management saw the deceleration internally ahead of the sell side. Read against that signal, the analyst debate over whether this is 2022 again argues about the shape of a recovery. The April posture points instead to a company positioning for a softer print rather than a beat-and-raise.

Hungary Lifts The Cap On Its 30% Production Incentive, Reopening Europe's No. 2 Hub
The rebate is 30 percent, extendable to 37.5 percent by counting 7.5 percent of non-Hungarian costs, and Hungary has hosted F1: The Movie, the Dune films and The Brutalist.
CEO Summary
Hungary's new government removed the cap on new registrations for its film production incentive, a 30% cash rebate extendable to 37.5% by counting 7.5% of non-Hungarian costs. The cap, imposed last year by the outgoing administration, had blocked new entrants to Europe's second-biggest production hub.
Why This Matters
The payment mechanism was never the risk: the rebate runs through a state-guaranteed collection account operated by the National Film Institute, so productions already registered kept their claims intact through the cap year. What changed twice in twelve months was access for new entrants, which makes Hungary a queue-position question rather than a payment-risk question. The advantage goes to productions that register the moment a project firms up, not at budget lock.
Specific Implication
Productions budgeting against Hungary's incentive, or any incentive regime with a recent history of political change, should build contractual protection for the possibility of a rebate cap or withdrawal mid-production. Hungary's cap was imposed and reversed within about a year.
The Facts
ItemDetail
The changeHungary's new government removes the cap on new registrations for the film incentive; applications can resume
What was cappedThe cap was introduced last year by the outgoing administration — a block on new entrants to the scheme
GovernmentPéter Magyar's new government, reaffirming support for the sector
The incentive30% cash rebate, extendable to 37.5% of eligible spend by including 7.5% of non-Hungarian costs
MechanismCash rebate via post-financing, state-guaranteed through the collection account operated by the National Film Institute
PositionEurope's second-biggest production hub; first country in Central Europe to introduce an incentive, in 2004
Named officialErvin Nagy, state secretary for culture: "Today, we have opened a new chapter… by lifting the blockage on the tax incentive system that ensures the smooth and predictable operation of the Hungarian film industry. This decision was made in consultation with industry stakeholders and with the support and trust of the Ministry of Finance."

The relaunch adds 3,500 hours of Versant-owned content and Bundesliga rights via a new USA Sports deal, after AVOD monthly active users grew more than 300 percent year over year.
CEO Summary
Versant is unifying Fandango into a single brand spanning free ad-supported streaming, movie ticketing and premium rentals, targeting Tubi, Pluto TV and Roku Channel. The launch adds 20%+ more titles and hours, including 3,500 hours of Versant-owned content, plus Bundesliga rights via a USA Sports deal.
Why This Matters
Fandango's claim to be the only brand combining free streaming, ticketing and rentals points at the actual differentiator. Ticketing gives the free tier a theatrical-intent data signal Tubi and Pluto cannot see, and a promotion surface studios may value beyond its ad rates. If Versant executes on that link, the service competes on data and windowing position rather than catalog scale, the one axis where the incumbents cannot follow.

Fandango's expansion adds another active bidder for free-ad-supported library and back-end rights, relevant to any reversion or library licensing conversation currently in progress.

WGA Joins The Fight, Federal Judge Sets Friday Hearing: Paramount-WBD Merger Heads For A Pre-July-22 Showdown
Paramount answered the suit with a pledge of at least 30 movies a year with full 45-day theatrical windows, and hired Daniel Petrocelli, who beat DOJ's 2018 AT&T-Time Warner challenge.
CEO Summary
The Writers Guild of America sued Tuesday in the Northern District of California to block Paramount Skydance's $110B acquisition of Warner Bros. Discovery on a labor-monopsony theory, one day after 12 state AGs filed their antitrust suit. Judge P. Casey Pitts hears the states' emergency TRO motion Friday July 17 and is asked to rule before July 22, the window in which Paramount could otherwise close. Paramount responded with a public commitment to at least 30 movies a year with full 45-day theatrical windows.
Why This Matters
The number that travels beyond this deal sits in the Facts: under the states' "anticipated top-grossing" submarket definition, Paramount-WBD plus Disney would together control 59% of tentpole distribution. If Judge Pitts accepts that submarket as legally cognizable, the precedent constrains every future studio combination and puts Disney's own share within reach of the same theory. That gives the majors a structural reason to prefer this case settled rather than decided.
Specific Implication
Talent and representatives with active or pending deals at Paramount or Warner Bros. Discovery are negotiating with two companies that still compete with each other for material and talent. That dynamic changes once the merger closes.
The Facts
  • The Writers Guild of America (East + West jointly) sued in the Northern District of California on Tuesday July 14 to block Paramount Skydance's $110B acquisition of Warner Bros. Discovery, one day after 12 state AGs filed their own antitrust suit. The WGA complaint is a labor-market (monopsony) theory: fewer competitors would let the merged entity "lower costs by suppressing writers' wages and reducing output."
  • The state AGs' emergency-relief motion (TRO + preliminary injunction) was assigned to U.S. District Judge P. Casey Pitts, with a hearing set for Friday July 17 and a ruling asked for before July 22 — the window in which Paramount could otherwise close (the EU is also expected to rule around July 22).
  • The states allege three harmed markets: wide-release theatrical (combined ~27% share at the 600+ theater threshold), "anticipated top-grossing" blockbusters (~30% at the 3,000+ theater threshold; Paramount-WBD + Disney together = 59% of that submarket), and basic cable channel licensing (27% by affiliate fees, 34% by viewership).
  • Paramount's response: it will commit to "at least 30 movies a year, with full 45-day windows," continue commissioning from independent producers, maintain two distinct film studios under independent creative leadership, and argues its true competitive market includes Amazon, Apple and Netflix.
  • Legal teams: Paramount has retained Paul Clement and Jeff Kessler (alongside Daniel Petrocelli, who beat DOJ's AT&T–Time Warner challenge in 2018, and Makan Delrahim, that case's DOJ antitrust chief, now Paramount's CLO).
  • Antitrust read (William Kovacic, ex-FTC chair, GW Law): the states' complaint is "good enough to give Paramount and Warner Brothers a hard time"; the likely endgame is a settlement converting executives' verbal promises into a binding order plus concessions to protect the theatrical channel and possibly a no-workforce-reduction funding commitment (the merger targets $6B in cost savings).
· Deadline (Ted Johnson, Dominic Patten) + The Ankler/Richard Rushfield · 2026-07-14 / 2026-07-15 · https://deadline.com/2026/07/wga-sues-over-paramount-warner-bros-discovery-merger-1236982226/ · Provenance: filing/transcript (WGA complaint + state AG TRO motion) + named-exec-quote (Paramount statement, Kovacic) source ↗
Paramount–WBD Fight Escalates: Ellisons Sued Over Alleged Trump 'Side Deal,' Antitrust Hearing Friday, AG Nominee Grilled
The suit flags $24 billion from Saudi, Qatari and UAE funds set to own 38.5 percent of the combined company, with no CFIUS review since they get no board seats or votes.
CEO Summary
A Paramount shareholder sued David and Larry Ellison in Delaware Chancery Court to block the $111B WBD merger, alleging they promised private benefits to President Trump to clear regulatory barriers; Paramount denies any commitments were made. The suit landed a day after the state AGs' antitrust action and the WGA's labor suit, with Judge Pitts set to hear the emergency injunction motion Friday and rule before July 22. At his Senate confirmation hearing, AG nominee Todd Blanche confirmed he was part of the DOJ's June sign-off decision.
Why This Matters
The Blanche testimony is the piece with legal consequence. His acknowledgment that he was part of the June sign-off pairs with the WSJ report that senior leadership closed the probe before career staff who leaned toward a challenge could object. Together they degrade the DOJ clearance's persuasive value before Judge Pitts. A clearance that looks politically administered stops functioning as evidence the deal is lawful, which leaves Paramount defending the merger on the merits as if no federal review had blessed it.
The Facts
  • A Paramount shareholder (lead plaintiff Paul Robbins, repped by Thomas Law/Public Integrity Project/Freedom of the Press Foundation) sued David and Larry Ellison in Delaware Chancery Court on Tuesday, seeking to block the $111B WBD merger and unspecified damages. The suit alleges the Ellisons promised "illegal private benefits to President Trump to remove federal regulatory barriers" — including settling Trump's CNN legal claims to "funnel cash" to him and firing CNN anchors Trump dislikes after the takeover. Trump is not named as a defendant.
  • Paramount's response: the suit "recycles allegations already reported and already addressed," and "no commitments from either David or Larry Ellison have been made to any government body… regarding the future of CNN or any other news property." It reiterated the deal "stands on its own merits."
  • The complaint landed a day after 12 Democratic state AGs (led by California's Rob Bonta) filed a federal antitrust suit; the WGA also sued on labor grounds. The AG case was assigned to U.S. District Judge P. Casey Pitts, who must rule on the states' emergency TRO/preliminary-injunction motion before July 22; a hearing is set for Friday (July 17). A granted TRO would freeze the deal for weeks. (Deadline also reported Paramount sought recusal of an initially-assigned judge, hence the reassignment.)
  • The states' market-share case: the combined entity would hold ~27% of wide-release theatrical (600+ theater threshold) and ~30% of top-grossing theatrical (3,000+ threshold); Paramount-WBD + Disney together would control 59% of the top-grossing market; ~27–34% of basic cable. Antitrust experts (William Kovacic, ex-FTC chair) call the complaint strong enough to force a serious fight and predict a settlement with binding commitments over the spoken CNN/journalism promises, possibly workforce guarantees (vs. the merger's $6B cost-savings target). Paramount's team: Paul Clement and Jeff Kessler.
  • At his Senate confirmation hearing Wednesday, AG nominee Todd Blanche (Trump's former personal lawyer, acting AG) told Sen. Cory Booker "I was part of that decision" on the DOJ's June 12 sign-off but claimed "no idea" whether career Antitrust Division staff recommended closing the probe. The WSJ reported senior leadership closed the investigation before career staffers — who leaned toward challenging the deal — could object. Booker also pressed Blanche on attending an April Paramount dinner honoring Trump while the DOJ probe was open.
  • Separately, the suit flags no visible CFIUS review despite $24B from Saudi, Qatari and UAE sovereign funds that would own 38.5% of the combined company (Paramount says no board seats/voting shares, so no CFIUS trigger).

Lionsgate Studios Draws Takeover Interest From Banijay and Mediawan
Lionsgate trades at roughly 26 times expected pretax profit, a premium that already turned away earlier suitors, while Mediawan's North Road unit already produced A24's $368 million hit Backrooms.
CEO Summary
Lionsgate Studios, market value around $3.8B with Hunger Games, John Wick and Twilight in its library, has drawn takeover interest from European groups Banijay and Mediawan, and has engaged an investment bank to assess inbound approaches. Pricing is the obstacle: Lionsgate trades at roughly 26x expected pretax profit, a premium that has already turned away earlier suitors.
Why This Matters
Lionsgate has engaged an investment bank to assess inbound approaches, a step that indicates the company is evaluating a sale rather than merely fielding unsolicited interest. Pricing has been the stated obstacle: Lionsgate trades at roughly 26x expected pretax profit, a premium Variety reports has already caused earlier suitors to walk away. Mediawan's North Road unit produced Backrooms, A24's highest-grossing release at $368M+ worldwide, giving it a recent track record operating scripted content rather than only acquiring libraries. Banijay's business is concentrated in unscripted formats (Big Brother, Survivor); an acquisition of Lionsgate would be its first entry into scripted film and TV at this scale.
Specific Implication
Talent and representatives with participation, sequel, or reversion terms tied to Hunger Games, John Wick, or Twilight are dealing with a company actively assessing acquisition offers. That raises the relevance of change-of-control language in any paper signed before a deal is reached.
The Facts
  • Lionsgate Studios (market value ~$3.8B; library includes Hunger Games, John Wick, Twilight, and the $1B+ Michael Jackson biopic Michael) has drawn acquisition interest from European media groups Banijay and Mediawan. The Banijay interest was first reported by Reuters.
  • Lionsgate has engaged an investment bank to assess inbound approaches. No deal is guaranteed; the company could stay independent. Bolloré (Canal+ parent) is not bidding, per Variety.
  • Banijay (Big Brother, Survivor — mostly unscripted formats) is weighing a bid but may be delayed by its ongoing All3Media integration. Lionsgate would add scripted film/TV IP and a real studio/library.
  • Mediawan has been building in English-language markets via Plan B, See-Saw, and its recent North Road acquisition (whose Chernin group produced A24's Backrooms — now A24's highest-grossing release at $368M+ WW). Lionsgate would add mainstream theatrical franchises and library scale onto that U.S. base.
  • Pricing is the obstacle: Lionsgate trades at ~26x expected pretax profit, a premium to comparable studios (LSEG data via Reuters); earlier suitors reportedly walked over the valuation gap.
  • Corporate texture: director/shareholder Mark Rachesky this month moved his ~10% stake into a new fund backed by RenWave Kore — a firm founded in 2024 by ex-Elliott Management PM Cody Kittle, itself backed by Sequoia Heritage. Lionsgate, Banijay declined to comment to Reuters; Mediawan declined to Variety.

Warner Bros. Reshuffles Its 2027–28 Slate: 'The Batman Part II' Slides To Feb 2028, An Original Grabs Prime October
The Great Beyond's move followed a 70mm IMAX test screening at Regal Irvine; Batman Part II's own production start had already slipped five months, and its new date keeps IMAX.
CEO Summary
Warner Bros. moved its original sci-fi The Great Beyond (Glen Powell, Jenna Ortega) to October 1, 2027 with 70mm IMAX prints, bumping The Batman Part II off that date to February 18, 2028, and swapped two other 2027 dates. The moves come while the Paramount acquisition sits in antitrust litigation, with WB now dating films directly against its would-be parent.
Why This Matters
Handing an original the October 1 launchpad that produced Gravity, Joker, A Star Is Born and Dune while sliding the franchise sequel to a February holiday frame inverts the standard slate hierarchy. WB is treating the test-screened original as the premium-format bet and the IP title as the moveable piece. That is a read on where the studio now believes event value comes from, and a post-close Paramount regime would have to either ratify or unwind it.
The Facts
  • Warner Bros.' original sci-fi/fantasy The Great Beyond (Glen Powell, Jenna Ortega, Emma Mackey, Sophie Okonedo, Merritt Wever, Samuel L. Jackson) moves from Nov. 13, 2026 to Oct. 1, 2027 — the studio opted for 70mm IMAX prints after a Regal Irvine test screening. It is its director's first original feature in over a decade, teased at CinemaCon, and currently the only major-studio wide release on Oct. 1 (a launchpad that has housed Gravity, Joker, A Star Is Born, Dune).
  • The move bumps The Batman Part II off Oct. 1, 2027 to Feb. 18, 2028 (Presidents Day 4-day weekend). Its production start was delayed five months; the new date adds post time and keeps IMAX. Cast: Robert Pattinson, Andy Serkis, Colin Farrell, plus new additions Scarlett Johansson, Sebastian Stan, Charles Dance.
  • WB also swapped two 2027 dates: Sam Esmail's Panic Carefully (Julia Roberts, Elizabeth Olsen, Eddie Redmayne) Feb. 26 → April 9, 2027 in IMAX; New Line's Revenge of La Llorona April 9 → Feb. 26, 2027.
  • Context: all these moves come while the pending Paramount acquisition of WB is held up by antitrust suits (previously expected to close fall 2026). WB is now dating films against its would-be parent — Paramount already had films on Feb. 26 and April 9, 2027. A hint The Great Beyond was moving came when Amazon MGM shifted How to Rob a Bank from Labor Day to Nov. 13, 2026. Paramount Primal just dated its R-rated comedy Boys for Life to April 9, 2027.

FCC Sets Aug. 6 Vote to Repeal the National Broadcast Ownership Cap
Nexstar, blocked by a federal court from buying Tegna under the current cap, backs the repeal; Commissioner Anna Gomez calls it unlawful and says only Congress can change it.
CEO Summary
The FCC signaled it will vote August 6 to repeal the cap barring a single owner from controlling TV stations reaching more than 39% of U.S. markets, replacing it with case-by-case transaction review. Nexstar, blocked by a federal court from acquiring Tegna under the cap, backs the repeal; Democratic Commissioner Anna Gomez calls it unlawful, arguing only Congress can change a statutory cap.
Why This Matters
Replacing a bright-line cap with case-by-case review moves the constraint from rule to regulator. Transaction approval becomes discretionary. In a commission whose chairman argued the case in a Breitbart op-ed, that discretion converts station M&A into a political-alignment exercise, favoring buyers with administration relationships over buyers with the strongest balance sheets.
The Facts
  • The FCC signaled it will vote Aug. 6 to repeal the longstanding cap that bars a single owner from controlling TV stations reaching more than 39% of U.S. markets, replacing it with a case-by-case review of individual transactions.
  • Chairman Brendan Carr (op-ed in Breitbart) argued the cap is obsolete because national programmers already reach 100% of the country via their own streaming services and virtual MVPDs like YouTube TV, and cable/social/Netflix face no equivalent limit.
  • Immediate consequence: Nexstar — a top station owner (also owns the CW network and NewsNation) — has been blocked by a federal court from completing its acquisition of Tegna on grounds it would give Nexstar too much local-TV control. Nexstar backs the repeal.
  • Dissent + legal risk: Democratic FCC Commissioner Anna Gomez called it "unlawful," arguing Congress set the 39% cap in federal law and "only Congress" can raise or eliminate it — signaling likely litigation. The NAB (broadcaster trade group) applauded the move.
  • The FCC stressed repeal is not automatic deal approval: transactions exceeding the old 39% cap could still be denied if not in the public interest, or approved if they are.
· Variety (Brian Steinberg) · 2026-07-15 · https://variety.com/2026/tv/news/fcc-end-cap-national-broadcast-ownership-1236811655/ · Provenance: named-official statements (FCC Chairman Carr op-ed; Commissioner Gomez; NAB; Nexstar) + reported source ↗

State AGs Sue To Block Paramount-Warner Bros. Discovery Merger, Seek Emergency TRO
Hours after the suit landed, WBD's CEO filed to sell $59.5M in shares, a second sale this year after $114M in March, with several other top executives also selling the same day.
CEO Summary
California AG Rob Bonta and 11 other state AGs sued July 13 to block Paramount Skydance's $110-111B acquisition of Warner Bros. Discovery, then filed the same evening for a temporary restraining order and preliminary injunction in San Francisco federal court, warning the deal could close as soon as July 22. The DOJ cleared the deal in March. Hours after the suit landed, WBD's CEO filed to sell roughly $59.5M in shares.
Why This Matters
The $7M per day ticking fee that begins September 30, 2026 converts litigation delay itself into leverage for the states. Even a TRO fight Paramount ultimately wins prices in at roughly $210M a month once the fee starts running. That gives the plaintiffs settlement pressure the merits of the Clayton Act claims alone would not supply.
Specific Implication
Overall or first-look deals tied to WBD face renewal terms that could shift once a combined Paramount-WBD entity begins consolidating redundant agreements, a risk that exists only if the merger closes.
The Facts
  • California AG Rob Bonta and 11 other state AGs sued July 13 to block Paramount Skydance's $110-111B acquisition of Warner Bros. Discovery, then filed a motion the same evening for a temporary restraining order and preliminary injunction in federal court in San Francisco, warning Paramount could otherwise close as soon as July 22.
  • The suit alleges the merger violates the Clayton Act by concentrating three markets: wide-release theatrical distribution, "anticipated top-grossing"/tentpole theatrical distribution (a market Bonta argues is legally distinct), and basic cable channel licensing — giving the combined company 27% of wide-release distribution, 30% of the tentpole submarket, and 27% of the basic-cable bundle.
  • The merger agreement's outside date is March 4, 2027 (auto-extending to June 4, 2027 if antitrust review is pending), with a $7M/day ticking fee on Paramount starting September 30, 2026.
  • The DOJ already cleared the deal in March 2026; the EU is expected to rule around the same July 22 window. The UK's competition watchdog has also opened a formal investigation.
  • Same day, WBD's CEO filed to sell ~2.2M shares (~$59.5M) per an SEC filing — a second sale this year after ~$114M in March — with several other top WBD executives also selling.

Paramount Revives 'A Nightmare on Elm Street' Via Craven Estate Rights Reversion
The Craven estate regained the rights back in 2019 with an attorney-producer who specializes in this exact reclamation move and has already run it on other horror franchises.
CEO Summary
Paramount closed a deal for U.S. rights to Wes Craven's original A Nightmare on Elm Street screenplay, reclaimed by the Craven estate through copyright termination, the rule letting authors and estates reclaim rights 35 years after publication. The new film launches under Paramount's new genre label, while the franchise's historic studio home is mid-acquisition by Paramount itself.
Why This Matters
Termination reached only the original screenplay, not the seven sequels, the 2010 reboot, or the TV series the historic studio produced as derivative works. Paramount is therefore buying a fresh point of origin rather than a library. The new film must stand as a first entry, and any use of elements introduced in the later films would still route through the legacy rights holder.
Specific Implication
Any writer or estate holding an older credit on a franchise property still generating interest should check whether the 35-year termination window has opened, since the Craven estate's reclamation converted directly into a new studio deal.
The Facts

A stacked federal-plus-state structure is what would actually move large-budget production math back into competitive range with UK and Georgia rebate regimes. Single-jurisdiction credits have not done that on their own, which makes the bill's stacking mechanics, not passage alone, the variable that determines whether production flight reverses.
Specific Implication

Music Industry Rolls Out "AI-Generated" vs "AI-Assisted" Labels — With A Data Point That Should Alarm All Of Hollywood
Deezer reports 44% of all new music on its platform is AI-generated and Apple Music says more than a third of its tracks are "100% AI," the backdrop for the new voluntary labeling scheme.
CEO Summary
A coalition including the RIAA, IFPI, the Grammys, and SAG-AFTRA introduced two voluntary labels for sound recordings: "AI-Generated" for works where AI is the entirety or primary portion, and "AI-Assisted" for substantially human work with AI elements. The startling data alongside the standard: Deezer reports 44% of all new music on its platform is AI-generated, and Apple Music reports more than a third of its tracks are "100% AI."
Why This Matters
The IFPI and RIAA framing of the labels as "an immediately understandable and easily scalable approach to transparency" fixes the industry's posture as disclosure rather than restriction. The standard creates a marketing tier for human-made work while erecting no barrier to AI volume. That is the baseline any film or TV guild negotiating AI-credit language will now be measured against.
Specific Implication
The labeling framework covers the sound recording but not lyrics, composition, music videos, or cover art, leaving those categories without an equivalent disclosure standard. That gap is relevant to any film or TV negotiation over AI-credit or disclosure language that might otherwise look to the music framework as a template.
The Facts
  • A coalition — RIAA, IFPI, the Grammys, SAG-AFTRA, the Human Artistry Campaign (plus A2IM, WIN, IMPALA) — introduced two voluntary labels for sound recordings: "AI-Generated" (AI used for the entirety or primary portion) and "AI-Assisted" (substantially human, AI for some expressive elements).
  • The icons are designed for adoption by digital music services and distributors. They do not yet cover AI use in lyrics, composition, music videos, or cover art.
  • The eye-opening data: Deezer reported 44% of all new music on its platform is AI-generated; Apple Music reported more than one-third of its tracks are "100% AI."
  • IFPI/RIAA leadership: "Fans want to know whether and how generative AI has been used… these labels will provide an immediately understandable and easily scalable approach to transparency."

None of it reaches the consent default a platform sets at launch, which means the only lever available against an opt-out design at the moment it ships is public pressure on the platform itself.
Specific Implication
Any client with a public Instagram profile is opted into Muse by default and must actively opt out to prevent the tool from generating content from that likeness.
The Facts

Banijay & All3Media Complete $8B Merger, Creating World's Largest Independent Producer
Nearly 200 production labels now sit under one 50/50 Banijay-RedBird IMI ownership, closing the same week as Comcast/Sky's $2.1B ITV deal reshapes distribution on the other side of the market.
CEO Summary
Banijay Entertainment and RedBird IMI's All3Media completed their merger, creating the world's largest independent producer: a London-based group at roughly $8B enterprise value with €7.4B in pro-forma revenue, nearly 200 production labels, and a catalog exceeding 265,000 hours including The Traitors, Big Brother, MasterChef, and Peaky Blinders. Ownership is a 50/50 JV between Banijay Group and RedBird IMI.
Why This Matters
The ownership detail carries the structural weight. RedBird IMI is RedBird Capital plus Abu Dhabi's IMI, which places Gulf sovereign-linked capital in joint control of the largest production group assembled outside a studio or streamer. Content supply at this scale now answers to an investor class with no legacy media balance sheet to protect, and with return horizons and sensitivities different from the strategic buyers who previously drove production consolidation.
Specific Implication
Producers and buyers negotiating with a Banijay- or All3Media-owned label are now dealing with a single ownership group across close to 200 labels rather than separately owned companies.
The Facts
  • Banijay Entertainment and RedBird IMI's All3Media completed their merger today, forming a London-based production-distribution group that keeps the Banijay Entertainment name. Combined enterprise value ~$8B (equity ~$5B); pro-forma 2024 revenue €7.4B ($8.5B); €50M ($57M) in targeted cost savings.
  • The combined entity holds a catalog exceeding 265,000 hours across nearly 200 production companies/labels (Banijay ~130 worldwide; All3Media ~40, UK/US/Germany-weighted). Franchises include The Traitors, Big Brother, MasterChef, Peaky Blinders, Midsomer Murders.
  • Ownership is a 50/50 JV between Banijay Group and RedBird IMI (RedBird Capital + Abu Dhabi's IMI), consolidated by Banijay Group. Each legacy company brought its own distribution arm, now under one roof.
  • The deal was announced in March 2026 and cleared regulatory review to close today. It lands the same week as Comcast/Sky's $2.1B ITV acquisition, both reshaping the international TV market.

The Get-Big-or-Die Era of European TV: Sky Buys ITV for $2.1B
Sky's £1.6B ITV deal excludes ITV Studios, which spins off separately with a Sky programming commitment of at least £2.1B for 2028-2032; two days later the BBC confirmed its own Channel 4 streaming talks.
CEO Summary
Comcast-owned Sky agreed to acquire ITV's broadcasting and streaming operations for £1.6B ($2.1B), combining Britain's largest pay-TV operator with its largest commercial free-to-air broadcaster; ITV Studios spins off as a standalone listed content business with a ≥£2.1B Sky programming commitment for 2028-2032. Two days later the BBC confirmed talks with Channel 4 about a combined streaming platform, and parallel consolidation is moving in Germany, Italy, and Spain.
Why This Matters
Regulators cleared the Sky-ITV combination, RTL's acquisition of Sky Deutschland, and MediaForEurope's cross-border stake-building in Spain, Italy and Portugal within a compressed period. The BBC confirmed talks with Channel 4 on a combined streaming platform two days after the Sky-ITV announcement. The pattern spans multiple national markets and ownership groups rather than a single deal.

A separate model has emerged in France. TF1 and, separately, France Télévisions and M6 signed carriage deals placing their channels and on-demand content inside Netflix's and Amazon's interfaces rather than merging with another broadcaster. RTVE signed a comparable deal with Amazon in Spain. Both the consolidation path and the carriage-deal path reduce the number of independently owned broadcasters operating without a merger partner or platform agreement.
Specific Implication
Anyone selling rights or formats into these markets now negotiates against fewer independently owned counterparties.
The Facts
  • Comcast-owned Sky agreed to acquire ITV's broadcasting and streaming operations for £1.6B ($2.1B) — combining Britain's largest pay-TV operator with its largest commercial free-to-air broadcaster. The deal excludes ITV Studios (Coronation Street, Love Island), which spins off as a standalone London-listed content business; Sky committed to ≥£2.1B ($2.8B) in ITV Studios programming spend for 2028–2032.
  • Two days later, the BBC said it is in talks with Channel 4 to combine streaming into a single British "sovereign platform."
  • The wave is Europe-wide: Germany's RTL cleared to buy Comcast's Sky Deutschland; the Berlusconi-family MediaForEurope controls ProSiebenSat.1 plus assets in Spain/Italy/Portugal.
  • The alternative model (France): rather than merge, TF1 struck a carriage deal to distribute through Netflix; France Télévisions and M6 signed similar deals with Amazon Prime Video, and Amazon added RTVE in Spain. Regulators who blocked such tie-ups for two decades are now clearing them, deciding global streamers/ad giants — not domestic TV consolidation — are the primary competitive threat.

  • The abruptness points to friction: an industry source told Deadline "the Disney culture didn't match," and that Gandler was "frustrated with what seemed to be Disney's almost intention to not grow the vMVPD business."
  • Fubo + Hulu + Live TV entered 2026 with 6.2M combined subscribers, far behind YouTube TV (10M+), which is poised to become the No. 1 US pay-TV operator as Comcast and Charter fade. Fubo recently completed a reverse stock split; stock was flat post-news at ~$9.56.

USMNT-Belgium Draws 33.1M on Fox, Most-Watched Soccer Telecast in US History
Fox's 33.1M-viewer USMNT-Belgium telecast was the biggest US network audience since Super Bowl LX's 125.6M and the most-watched non-NFL telecast since the 2016 World Series Game 7.
CEO Summary
Fox's coverage of the July 6 USMNT-Belgium World Cup Round-of-16 match averaged 33.1M viewers, peaking at 41M, the most-watched soccer telecast in US history and the biggest US telecast on any network since the Super Bowl. Fox's full Round of 16 averaged 14.5M, up 216% from 2022, while Telemundo/Peacock posted the highest total-day Spanish-language audience in US history.
Why This Matters
The audience arrived for a match the US lost 4-1 to end its tournament, and the record was set in a round of 16, not a final. That is the detail rights buyers will price. Demand at this scale was not contingent on a winning home side, which supports valuing US World Cup rights on tournament appeal rather than discounting them for USMNT performance risk.
The Facts
  • Fox's coverage of the July 6 USMNT-Belgium World Cup Round-of-16 match averaged 33,086,000 viewers — the most-watched soccer telecast in US history, peaking at 41.03M. The US lost 4-1 and was eliminated.
  • It was the most-watched telecast on any US network since Super Bowl LX (125.6M, Feb. 8), and the most-watched non-NFL telecast since World Series Game 7 in 2016 (40.05M).
  • Fox's full Round-of-16 averaged 14.465M (most-watched English-language R16 in US history, up 216% from 2022). Telemundo/Peacock ranked No. 1 in Spanish-language total-day for 25 straight days; July 5 was the highest total-day Spanish-language audience in US history (3.6M).
  • This is the 2026 World Cup hosted across the US/Canada/Mexico; the 39-day tournament (June 11) concludes July 19. Rights held by Fox (English) and Telemundo/Peacock (Spanish).

California's New Tax-Credit Round Opens To Animation; Studios Take Two-Thirds
Disney/Pixar and DreamWorks Animation took $71M of California's newly-opened animation tax credit across four films, out of a $187M pool where studios captured roughly two-thirds of the total.
CEO Summary
California allocated $187M in film and TV tax credits across 41 projects, opening the program to animated features for the first time: Disney/Pixar and DreamWorks Animation took $71M across four films. L.A. movie location-shooting days jumped 52% year over year in Q1 per FilmLA, and studios captured roughly two-thirds of the total allocation.
Why This Matters
The first animation allocations went to a Pixar title at $26.2M, a Disney title at $18.5M, and two DreamWorks titles at $19M and $7.8M. The new eligibility category therefore functioned in its debut as a retention payment to the state's two largest animation employers rather than recapture of work that had left. That is a defensible use of an anti-runaway program, but it is a different return profile from the location-shooting gains cited in the same report, where the credit can claim to have moved production back.
Specific Implication
Independent animation and live-action projects are competing for the remaining roughly one-third of the credit pool after studio allocations, a narrower share than the program's total figure implies.
The Facts
  • California allocated its latest round of film/TV tax credits — $187M across 41 projects. L.A. movie location-shooting days were +52% YoY in Q1 (FilmLA).
  • The program now opens to animated features for the first time. Animated pics from Disney/Pixar and DreamWorks Animation took $71M across 4 films (a Pixar untitled at $26.2M; a Disney title at $18.5M; two DWA titles at $19M and $7.8M).
  • Live-action allocations: WB $41M (untitled), Disney/20th $8.2M (untitled comedy), plus an independent pickup. Studios captured ~two-thirds of the total allocation.
  • Context: this is the expanded, better-funded California incentive (the state boosted its program to counter runaway production); the location-day jump suggests it is pulling shoots back to L.A.
