TF1's CEO said the deal hit audience targets originally set for 18 months in under three weeks, and ad sales on Netflix-carried shows held up. It is Netflix's first partnership with a free-to-air national broadcaster.
Why This Matters
Several TF1 titles reaching Netflix's most-watched lists for France within the first weeks shows the value running in both directions: Netflix acquired chart-topping local engagement without commissioning it, at carriage economics rather than production cost. If a domestic broadcaster's existing output can occupy the platform's local charts, carriage becomes a substitute for a portion of local-originals spend, a stronger rationale for the model than the distribution fees themselves.
Specific Implication
A broadcaster or format owner with a strong domestic audience has a carriage-deal alternative to a merger, evidenced by TF1's reported results and Amazon's parallel deals with France Télévisions, M6 and RTVE.
The Facts
  • French commercial broadcaster TF1 reports record streaming figures three weeks into its carriage deal with Netflix, which puts TF1's live channels and TF1+ on-demand content inside Netflix's interface in France.

  • Week 1 unique daily streams for TF1 shows jumped 16%; several TF1 titles hit Netflix's most-watched lists for France. TF1's CEO said the deal hit audience targets originally set for an 18-month timeframe in under three weeks; TF1 said ad sales on its Netflix-carried shows were "excellent" and comparable to TF1+ (no figures given).
  • This is the first time Netflix has partnered with a free-to-air national broadcaster; it is being eyed as a model for other territories/streamers. Amazon has parallel carriage deals (France Télévisions, M6 in France; RTVE in Spain).

As Streamers Battle Churn, AI Start-Up Versus Sells Engagement-As-Retention
Versus claims content-start rates above 53%, against a cited 2% industry average, deployed on library titles like The Sopranos and Oppenheimer across Disney+, Paramount+ and HBO Max.
CEO Summary
AI startup Versus came out of stealth selling interactive "play-along" overlays, trivia, polls, predictions, and leaderboards, layered on live sports and series to keep viewers on-platform. Named partners include Disney+, Paramount+, HBO Max, and the NFL, with deployments across library titles from The Sopranos to Oppenheimer. The company claims 16% longer sessions and retention lifts of 4.5% to 9.5%.
Why This Matters
The detail separating this from a routine vendor story is FourPlay.ai, Versus's own direct-to-consumer product, launched while the company operates inside competing platforms on a fee-share model. A supplier monetizing engagement mechanics across rival services accumulates cross-platform learning no single client can see in full. The D2C launch signals intent to apply that learning under its own brand, the standard trajectory from tool vendor to competitor.
Specific Implication
Versus's overlays run on top of content licensed from multiple rights holders, including Lionsgate and the NFL. Which party controls and monetizes that interactive layer is a question distinct from the underlying content rights and is worth addressing directly in licensing terms.
The Facts
  • Versus, out of stealth, sells AI overlays that turn passive viewing into interactive "play-along" experiences — trivia, polls, predictions, leaderboards, prizes — layered on live sports, series, and articles, aimed at keeping viewers on-platform instead of bailing to TikTok/Instagram.
  • Named platform partners: Disney+, Paramount+, HBO Max, the NFL, plus content providers Lionsgate and Reddit. Deployed across current series and library (The Sopranos, Game of Thrones, The Wire) and films (Dune, Oppenheimer, Superman).
  • Backers include a heavyweight tech/media investor group. Founded 2019 by tech entrepreneur John Vitti (CEO).
  • Claimed results: 16% greater session lengths; 4.5–9.5% retention lifts; content-start rates >53% (vs a 2% "industry average"); ~70% week-over-week retention among engaged users. Revenue via platform-fee share, sponsorship/ad integrations. First D2C move: FourPlay.ai (live-sports-focused).

A Top Talent Agency Calls Out Meta's Muse AI Image Tool Over Opt-Out Likeness Defaults
Meta's Muse Image tool, launched July 7, lets any user generate AI remixes of a public account with no consent step. Meta's same-night rebuttal kept opt-out as the default, not opt-in.
CEO Summary
CAA publicly demanded Wednesday night that Meta make privacy protection the default on its new Muse Image AI tool, which lets any Instagram user generate AI "remixes" of anyone with a public account, no consent step required. Meta rebutted the same night and defended its opt-out design. This is the second likeness confrontation between a top agency and a Big Tech generative-AI launch in under a year, after OpenAI's Sora.
Why This Matters
CAA's statement asks Meta to "enable individuals to opt-in," while Meta's quoted defense is that adult users "can opt out with just a couple clicks." Both sides concede the mechanism works. The only contested question is who carries the burden of acting, which makes this a fight over the default allocation of consent rather than the tool's capability. Neither side can settle without reversing a stated position.
Specific Implication
The practical expectation is that likeness protection on major platforms stays opt-out unless legislation or litigation forces a change.
The Facts
  • CAA publicly demanded Wednesday night that Meta make privacy protection the DEFAULT setting on its newly launched Muse Image AI tool, not an opt-out a user must find and activate. The July 7 Muse Image rollout lets any Instagram user tag a public account and have the AI generate "remixes" (deepfake-style images) of that person with no consent step.
  • CAA's roster includes major film stars and digital-first talent whose public accounts are exposed to the tool by default.
  • CAA's statement: "No one's name, image, likeness, voice, or creative work should be used by any third party, including AI models, without clear, documented consent... We call on Meta to make protection the default on Muse Image, not the exception, and enable individuals to opt-in if they want to allow usage of their image or likeness for AI content creation."
  • Meta's same-night rebuttal: "We built Muse Image with strong controls and safety guardrails from day one. Private accounts and those belonging to users under 18 are automatically excluded and adult users with public accounts can opt out with just a couple clicks. We will take action against any content that violates our Community Standards."
  • This follows the same pattern as the rocky rollout of OpenAI's Sora, which drew the same agency-side pushback over consent defaults.

Oregon's AG wants documents on Paramount's role drafting the DOJ's own statement backing the deal, internally referenced as "Project Warrior," and a 60-day delay; a hearing is set for Monday.
CEO Summary
Oregon's attorney general asked a state court to delay Paramount's $110B acquisition of Warner Bros. Discovery by 60 days and to compel documents on Paramount's lobbying of federal officials, including any role in drafting the DOJ statement supporting the deal. The DOJ cleared the transaction last month; Paramount has said it won't close before July 22, the EU decision deadline. California and other state AGs are reportedly weighing their own challenges.
Why This Matters
Rayfield's argument that a DOJ approval obtained through "a corrupt bargain" would inform Oregon's own investigative file attacks the legitimacy of the federal clearance rather than the merger's competitive effects. That framing makes the document fight the live risk independent of the delay request. Material produced in a state discovery proceeding becomes available to every other attorney general considering a challenge, whether or not the delay is granted.
Specific Implication
Closing-date-contingent deal terms on the WBD side remain unresolved despite DOJ clearance. Oregon's motion, a potential California challenge, and the open EU deadline each still stand between the deal and an actual close.
The Facts
  • Oregon AG Dan Rayfield filed a motion asking a Multnomah County Circuit Court judge to delay Paramount's closing of its proposed $110B acquisition of Warner Bros. Discovery by 60 days, and to compel Paramount to turn over documents on its lobbying of federal officials — including any role in drafting the DOJ's public statement supporting the deal (referenced internally as "Project Warrior"). Hearing set for Monday.
  • The DOJ's Antitrust Division cleared the transaction last month. Paramount has said it won't close before July 22 (the EU regulatory decision deadline). UK Culture Secretary Lisa Nandy has said she is "minded to intervene" but has not made a formal move, though the UK's actual power to block the deal is legally unclear.
  • California AG Rob Bonta and other state AGs are reportedly weighing their own challenge.
  • Rayfield: "Oregonians have a real stake in this deal... Paramount had every opportunity to hand over records and answer a few basic questions. Instead, it is trying to run out the clock and evade scrutiny." His motion argues that if DOJ approval were "the product of a corrupt bargain," that would inform Oregon's reading of its own investigative file.
  • Paramount's court filing objects to the document requests as disproportionate, privileged, and infringing on First Amendment/lobbying rights, calling the lobbying activity "wholly irrelevant" to Oregon's antitrust review.

It is Netflix's third straight July 4 tentpole to underwhelm, and the platform's weekly Top 9 film view count of 67M was the lowest in the outlet's tracked window since early May. The softness mirrors the same weekend's theatrical malaise.
Why This Matters
The reception data cuts against a quality explanation. Enola Holmes 3's 52% RT audience score sits well above The Old Guard 2's 33%, yet the newer title opened roughly 17M views lower over the same five-day window. When a better-received film in the same holiday slot opens at barely half the level, the decline reads as a shift in how the platform's tentpole premieres perform, not a run of weak titles.
Specific Implication
Compensation or bonus structures pegged to Netflix-reported view counts rely on figures the platform generates and reports itself, without independent audit.
The Facts
  • Enola Holmes 3 (Millie Bobby Brown) debuted to 20.7M views over its first 5 days on Netflix — the softest opening of the three-film franchise on a like-for-like basis: Enola Holmes 2 did 29.6M (3-day, 2022), Damsel 35.3M (3-day, 2024), The Electric State 25.6M (3-day, 2025). The 20.7M figure is a 5-day total, making the actual gap wider than the raw numbers suggest.
  • Netflix's last three July 4-weekend tentpoles have each underwhelmed: Back in Action (41M 5-day, 2024, RT audience 75%), The Old Guard 2 (37.5M 5-day, 2025, RT audience 33%), and now Enola Holmes 3 (20.7M 5-day, 2026, RT audience 52%).
  • Enola Holmes 3 was also outpaced domestically by week 2 of Little Brother (12.7M views, #1 in the US) — the new tentpole didn't lead its own platform's US chart at debut.
  • Global Top 9 Netflix Films view count for the week ending July 5 was 67M — the lowest weekly total in the outlet's tracked window since early May (which ranged 71.7M–96.2M), continuing a multi-week decline.
  • The outlet explicitly frames this as mirroring the same-weekend theatrical malaise, while noting the softness has not shown up internationally to the same degree.

Why This Matters
The structural detail in Lang's account is that Allen & Co. profits as M&A adviser regardless of how the deals it incubates perform. A convening institution paid on volume rather than outcomes biases the room toward transacting, and the roster of value-destroying mergers Lang catalogs is what that incentive structure would be expected to produce.
Specific Implication
Agreements whose value depends on a counterparty's current ownership or leadership carry elevated change-of-control risk in the period following the conference.
The Facts

Allen & Co. profits as M&A adviser regardless of outcome.

  • Thesis line: "history shows us that the best deal may be the one you didn't make."

Six of the seven declining second seasons scored higher critically than their first, and One Piece Season 2 hit 100 percent on Rotten Tomatoes, yet still lost viewers.
CEO Summary
The Wrap found that of seven 2026 Netflix second seasons of once-buzzy shows, four dropped 40% or more in viewership, with A Good Girl's Guide to Murder down 76% and A Man on the Inside S2 never charting. The decline is not a quality problem: six of seven scored higher critically than their first seasons. Netflix declined comment; it canceled nine first-season shows by June.
Why This Matters
The diagnosed causes, multi-year gaps between seasons, split or confusing release schedules, and the binge model, are distribution decisions rather than creative ones. That locates the retention problem inside Netflix's own scheduling machinery. A second season's audience is being shaped more by how the platform releases it than by what its creators deliver.
Specific Implication
Critical reception and season-over-season audience retention moved in opposite directions across the tracked titles. A second-season renewal on Netflix is not evidence the platform intends to sustain or grow a show's audience past its first season.
The Facts
  • The Wrap found a repeated pattern: of seven 2026 Netflix second seasons of once-buzzy shows, four dropped ≥40% in viewership — "A Good Girl's Guide to Murder" (-76%), "The Four Seasons," "Avatar: The Last Airbender" (~-60%), "Running Point." "A Man on the Inside" S2 never charted at all after a strong S1.
  • The decline is not a quality problem — six of seven S2s scored higher critically than their S1s ("One Piece" S2 hit 100% on RT). It extends beyond sophomore seasons: "The Night Agent" S3 launched to 8.4M views, down from S2's 13.9M.
  • Diagnosed causes: multi-year gaps between seasons (Avatar, One Piece ~3 years), confusing/split release schedules, binge model, and content glut. Netflix canceled nine first-season shows by June 2026.
  • Evan Shapiro: Netflix has become "the McDonald's of streaming" — "consumers' only loyalty is to the shows, not the platforms." An insider notes decline is prevalent across all of TV; a comparable case cited is a strong-S1 series on a rival streamer quietly canceled after a low-fanfare S2.

Director Lauren Montgomery publicly objected in March, saying the move "might give the impression that the quality wasn't sufficient" and that the film "deserves to be seen on a big screen."
Why This Matters
The confirmation arrives with a new trailer, an 18-day runway to the premiere, and a San Diego Comic-Con appearance set for later this month. That rollout shape shows Paramount+ substituting a single owned fan event for the theatrical marketing campaign the film no longer has. The platform is spending convention heat rather than media dollars to launch the title.
Specific Implication
A studio's stated intention to release a project theatrically does not bind that outcome. The reversal on Avatar Aang, made despite the director's public objection, indicates a theatrical window needs to be a contractual guarantee rather than an expressed intent when it matters to a production.
The Facts
  • Paramount+ will premiere the animated Avatar Aang: The Last Airbender globally on July 25, 2026 — confirming it has dropped its theatrical release, which had been dated Oct. 9. A theatrical skip was reported last winter; today makes it official, with a new trailer.
  • From Avatar Studios (Michael Dante DiMartino, Bryan Konietzko). Voice cast: Eric Nam (Aang), Dave Bautista, Jessica Matten, Román Zaragoza, Steven Yeun (Zuko), Dionne Quan. Lauren Montgomery directs. Will appear at San Diego Comic-Con this month.
  • Director Montgomery publicly pushed back in March: the theatrical→streaming move "might give the impression that the quality wasn't sufficient, but that couldn't be farther from the truth… This movie deserves to be seen on a big screen."

Sky To Take Over ITV In $2.1B Deal That Reshapes British Television
Sky separately locked a six-year, £2.1 billion output deal with ITV Studios through 2032, a commitment worth more than the £1.6 billion it paid for ITV's network itself.
CEO Summary
Comcast/NBCUniversal-owned Sky has agreed to acquire ITV's broadcast network and ITVX streaming operations for £1.6B ($2.1B), confirmed to shareholders Monday. ITV Studios, the production arm, is not part of the deal and stays independent, with Sky locking a separate £2.1B output deal through 2032. It is the second mega-consolidation of traditional TV in a month, after Paramount and Warner Bros. Discovery.
Why This Matters
Even combined, Sky and ITV's 17.7 percent viewing share still trails YouTube's 18.6 percent, and the agreement keeps ITV Studios' flagship series free-to-air rather than moving them behind Sky's paywall. That structure shows what the acquisition is actually buying: advertising reach at national scale, not subscription exclusivity. It marks a shift in legacy-TV consolidation logic from paywall conversion toward defensive audience aggregation against the platforms.
Specific Implication
A production arm structured as a formally separate entity from an acquired network can have its supply agreement preserved and extended through the acquisition, as ITV Studios' £2.1B output deal shows.
The Facts
  • Comcast/NBCUniversal-owned Sky has agreed to acquire ITV's television network and streaming (ITVX) operations for £1.6B ($2.1B). ITV confirmed to shareholders Monday morning. Structure: £1.2B upfront + £200M payable H1 2028, contingent on ITV ad revenue hitting £1.7B next year; ~£950M cash return (25p/share) to ITV shareholders.
  • ITV Studios (the production arm — Love Island, Coronation Street, I'm a Celebrity) is NOT part of the deal and stays independent. Sky struck a separate £2.1B output deal with ITV Studios through 2032 to keep producing those series (they will not move behind Sky's paywall), and is selling Bake Off producer Love Productions to ITV Studios in a £200M side-deal.
  • Combined Sky+ITV TV/streaming viewing share was 17.7% in May, just behind YouTube's 18.6% (Barb). ITV generated £2B revenue in 2025, ~32% commercial-viewing share; ITVX has ~16.5M monthly active users.
  • Sits under NBCUniversal, which Comcast is spinning out as a standalone listed company. Nearly nine-month courtship since ITV's November notice. Follows the Paramount–Warner Bros. Discovery merger moving toward completion.

Behind The 'Supergirl' Bomb: Competing Cuts, Creative Differences
The studio ran a nine-day additional shoot and reversed a needle-drop into a cover critics panned. Its $37.1 million opening came in below 2024's Joker: Folie à Deux at $37.6 million.
CEO Summary
THR's postmortem reports Supergirl, budgeted $170M to $180M, opened to $37.1M and collapsed 74% in weekend two after months of internal alarm: test scores never left the 60s, a March bakeoff pitting the director's cut against the studio's own cut sent scores down, and the studio took over post-production. It was DC Studios' first tentpole not written or directed by its lead filmmaker.
Why This Matters
Scores falling after the bakeoff is the detail that reframes the postmortem. When neither the director's cut nor the studio's improves the number, the problem sits upstream of the edit in the material that was greenlit. The months of post-production intervention were a contest over a film neither side could fix. That reading converts the failure from a creative-control dispute into a development miss, which is the harder problem for a studio betting its slate can scale beyond its lead filmmaker's own authorship.
Specific Implication
A director attached to a franchise tentpole without contractual final-cut protection can see the studio assume creative control during post-production when test scores decline.
The Facts
  • Supergirl (DC Studios/Warner Bros., dir. Craig Gillespie), budgeted $170M–$180M, opened to $37.1M — below 2024's Joker: Folie à Deux ($37.6M); second-weekend domestic collapse of -74%.
  • DC Studios knew for months the film wasn't working. In March they held a "bakeoff," testing the director's cut against the studio's own cut — and scores went DOWN. Test scores (out of 100) "never escaped the 60s," top score 70, across at least four test screenings (Dec 2025, Feb, March 2026).
  • The studio and director had creative differences (some insiders call it normal friction). After a just-okay December test, the studio took over post-production, ran a nine-day additional shoot, and reconfigured the climactic fight. Music was a key friction point (a studio-chosen needle-drop was reversed to a cover critics panned). Two editors — the director's longtime partner and the studio's — cut the film.
  • This was the studio's first tentpole not written/directed by its lead filmmaker — a key test of whether the reset DC universe can scale beyond him.

Supergirl's second worldwide weekend fell 70 percent, domestic down 74 percent, on a budget of $170 million to $186 million before P&A, even after crossing $100 million globally.
CEO Summary
Minions & Monsters opened to a franchise-low $61.4M five-day domestic, half of Despicable Me 4's start two years ago, but an $85M net cost and a $146.4M No.1 global weekend keep it profitable. Toy Story 5 reached $764.3M worldwide with a record Japan opening, while Supergirl crossed $100M global against a $170M-plus budget.
Why This Matters
The weekend's results separate on cost base, not demand. Family animation at a controlled budget absorbed its weakest domestic start and stayed profitable. The superhero entry's budget left no cushion once its audience evaporated in week two. The instructive comparison is Supergirl's $19 million second worldwide weekend set against Minions & Monsters' foreign-led frame. In a market this front-loaded and international-dependent, budget discipline has replaced opening size as the variable that decides which tentpoles make money.
Specific Implication
For participation talks on family-animation franchises, the lesson is where the money actually clears. With production costs at this scale and profitability carried by foreign grosses and merchandise, backend tied to global receipts and consumer products is worth more than domestic opening-weekend bonuses.
The Facts
  • Minions & Monsters (Universal, dir. Pierre Coffin) opened to a franchise-low $61.4M 5-day domestic — the lowest start ever for the Despicable Me/Minions franchise (Despicable Me 4 opened to $122M two years ago). Foreign saved it: $85M overseas weekend, $146.4M No.1 global weekend across 72 territories, $159.8M WW cume. On an $85M net production cost plus merchandise, Deadline judges it "perfectly fine profit-wise." China led at $16.4M. World Cup cited as a domestic headwind.
  • Toy Story 5 (Disney/Pixar, dir. Andrew Stanton) took a clear No.2 global with a $100M third weekend, $764.3M WW ($398M intl / $366.3M domestic). Highest-ever MPA opening weekend in Japan (~$14.6M).
  • Supergirl (WB/DC) crossed $100M global but on a $170M–$186M budget before P&A: a dismal $19M second worldwide weekend, -70%, domestic -74%.
  • Obsession (Focus/Universal) reached $403M WW in weekend eight. Disclosure Day (Universal) at $216.6M WW. Disney's live-action Moana looms next.

CEO Summary
Netflix filed an appeal with France's Council of State against new rules requiring streamers to boost investment in animation, documentaries and live performance, and is publicly calling for a cap on its total French investment obligations. Prime Video confirmed it is also appealing, with Disney+ reportedly joining. Netflix currently invests €250M a year in French content.
Why This Matters
The cap request is the escalation that matters. Obligations European regulators have treated as an uncapped cost of market access are being reframed as a negotiable ceiling. The Le Monde op-ed's argument that the rules fix the genre balance of the slate in law elevates the dispute from spending levels to editorial control. Set beside the parallel pushback in Belgium and Germany, the appeals read as a coordinated test of how much slate sovereignty streamers can reclaim. The French outcome will set the template other member states negotiate against.
Specific Implication
Producers relying on the French streamer investment mandate as a financing source should treat the pending appeals, not the original rule, as the current determinant of guaranteed streamer spend in France.
The Facts
  • Netflix filed an appeal before France's Council of State against new EU/French "diversity" rules requiring streamers to boost investment in animation, documentaries and live performance, and published an op-ed (Le Monde) making its case. It argues the rules "fix in law the exact genre balance of our slate" and apply only to streamers, not traditional broadcasters.
  • Prime Video confirmed it is also appealing; a French report says Disney+ is too. Netflix additionally wants a cap on overall investment obligations in France, warning that without one its role "could become reduced."
  • Netflix currently invests €250M/year in French content; says it has contributed €2B+ since 2014 (160+ local titles).
  • Pattern: Netflix lost a Belgium appeal in March; its EMEA chief pushed back on new German obligations in June (warned against "one size fits all" rights-retention rules benefitting PE/sovereign-wealth-backed indies). Netflix also appealed France's windowing rules last year — the reason it doesn't compete at Cannes.

Emmys Hunt for a 2027 Home Exposes How Far Awards-Telecast Rights Have Repriced Down, With YouTube Already Holding the Oscars
Emmys rights are valued around $10 million a year, up only slightly from just over $8 million now, while YouTube has already taken the Oscars from ABC starting 2029.
CEO Summary
Primetime Emmys rights expire after the September 2026 telecast on NBC, and the Television Academy is weighing a single buyer, the traditional four-network wheel, a larger wheel with streamers, or a cost-sharing simulcast for 2027 onward. Rights value is thought to be around $10M a year, networks say the telecast is no longer a major revenue generator, and YouTube already took the Oscars from ABC starting 2029.
Why This Matters
The newly floated simulcast, with multiple broadcasters and streamers sharing rights and production cost, is the structural admission. Exclusivity in an awards telecast no longer commands a premium, so the Academy is effectively selling cost relief rather than a rights asset. With Netflix downplaying solo-ownership interest and YouTube already holding the comparable marquee property, the only bidder class with strategic appetite for live awards is the platform legacy networks are losing share to. That caps any recovery in the rights price regardless of which structure the Academy picks.
The Facts
  • Primetime Emmys rights expire after the 2026 event (Sept 14, on NBC). The Television Academy is in talks over where the show airs from 2027 onward.
  • Options: single network/platform; the traditional four-network "wheel"; a larger wheel including streamers; or a newly floated simulcast across multiple broadcasters and streamers sharing rights and production cost.
  • Rights value is "thought to be around $10M per year" (current deal "just over $8M per year").
  • Networks are cautious because the telecast "is no longer a major revenue generator"; streamers including Netflix have downplayed solo-ownership interest. Notably, YouTube took the Oscars from Disney's ABC beginning 2029.

Crunchyroll Founder Launches AniBiz, A Global B2B Anime-Licensing Marketplace
Crunchyroll founder Kun Gao's new company Nakama launched the marketplace with Aniplex of America, Toei Animation, TOHO Global, Avex, Pony Canyon, TV Tokyo, TV Asahi and TBS already on board.
CEO Summary
Crunchyroll founder Kun Gao's new company Nakama launched AniBiz.com, a B2B marketplace for global anime licensing, with Aniplex of America, Toei Animation, TOHO Global, TV Tokyo and roughly 25 other top Japanese IP holders on board at launch. The global anime market is estimated to exceed $35B this year.
Why This Matters
The promise of territory-specific demand data is the real product: anime licensing has priced on private relationships precisely because neither side could see comparable terms. A marketplace that aggregates that information becomes the category's price-discovery layer. That a Crunchyroll founder is building it matters, since the person who consolidated anime's consumer distribution is now positioning to hold the data layer that sets what its licensing is worth.
Specific Implication
Licensors who list gain reach but surrender information: every transaction on the platform teaches the marketplace, not the rights holder, what territory demand is worth. A rights holder should therefore set data-ownership and data-access terms before listing, not after the first transactions have priced its catalog.
The Facts
  • Crunchyroll founder/former CEO Kun Gao's new company Nakama launched AniBiz.com, billed as "the first dedicated B2B marketplace built specifically for the global anime industry" — a centralized platform to streamline anime licensing across rights holders, distributors, merchandisers, retailers, and global buyers.
  • Launch licensors include Aniplex of America, Toei Animation, TOHO Global, Avex, Pony Canyon, TV Tokyo, TV Asahi, TBS and ~20 more top Japanese IP holders.
  • Framing: the global anime market is estimated to exceed $35B this year, with IP demand outrunning the "traditional, largely fragmented" licensing system. AniBiz promises "data-driven insights into market trends and territory-specific demand."

The director's cut tested better on song choice, pacing and the villain but ran 11 minutes longer, and still lost the studio's bakeoff by two points.
CEO Summary
The Hollywood Reporter details a formal March bakeoff between two competing cuts of Supergirl, one from the director and one assembled by studio leadership, after test scores stalled in the 60s across four screenings. The studio's cut won by two points and the film was never tested again; it opened to $37.1M on a $170M-$180M budget.
Why This Matters
The margin that decided the bakeoff was inside the noise of audience testing, yet it ended all further screening, and the studio installed its favored writer and regular editor to retool the film afterward. Taken together, the sequence indicates the release cut was selected by internal authority rather than by audience data, which reframes the opening as a governance outcome rather than a marketing or genre failure.
Specific Implication
A director attached to a studio-driven franchise picture should have final-cut and testing terms defined in the deal before production begins, rather than resolved informally during post-production.
The Facts
  • In March, the studio ran a formal bakeoff between two competing cuts of Supergirl — one from the film's director, one assembled by the studio's leadership — after test scores stalled in the 60s across four screenings and never cleared 70. The studio's cut won by only two points; the director's version tested better on song choice, pacing, and the villain, but was 11 minutes longer.
  • Reported creative differences between the studio's leadership and the director led to an unusually hands-on post-production process: the studio brought in a favored writer and its own regular editor to help retool the film. The film was never tested again after the bakeoff.

As Paramount-Warner Merger Enters Antitrust Crunch Time In Europe, Britain Holds Key To The September Deadline
EU concessions reportedly include divesting some kids' TV networks on top of the UIP exit. In the worst case, the companies may not legally merge until early 2027.
CEO Summary
Paramount's ~$110B acquisition of Warner Bros. Discovery has cleared the DOJ and nears EU clearance, with a Commission decision due July 22 after Paramount's June 30 concessions, including exiting its UIP distribution JV with Universal. The UK is now the primary roadblock: the culture secretary is "minded to intervene," and a phase-two referral could run five-plus months. Paramount targets an end-September close because a ticking fee owes WBD shareholders roughly $650M per quarter of slippage.
Why This Matters
The remedy package and the remaining objection do not line up. The concessions offered were built for competition review, distribution and channel divestitures of the kind Brussels prices. The UK's stated ground is media plurality, a public-interest test that asset sales of that type do not directly cure. The playbook that bought clearance elsewhere therefore has no equivalent purchase in London, and the parties' only real lever on the UK track is timeline management inside the 40-day phase-one window.
Specific Implication
The July 6 representations deadline is the last point at which the parties can shape the UK outcome by argument. Once a formal intervention notice issues, the closing date is governed by statutory review clocks rather than negotiation. That makes the days before July 6 the highest-leverage window left in the transaction.
The Facts
  • Paramount's ~$110B acquisition of Warner Bros. Discovery has cleared the U.S. DOJ and is nearing EU clearance after Paramount submitted concessions on June 30; the European Commission decides by July 22 whether to clear or open a phase-two probe.
  • The UK is now the primary roadblock. The culture secretary is "minded to intervene," calling the deal "unprecedented." Paramount has until July 6 to make final representations; a formal intervention notice (the likely outcome) would trigger Ofcom + CMA reviews (40 days), then a decision to clear or refer to a phase-two probe of 5+ months.
  • Paramount targets an end-September close, driven by a "ticking fee" to WBD shareholders of ~$650M per quarter the deal slips past Q3.
  • EU concessions reportedly include Paramount exiting its UIP international-distribution joint venture with Universal and divesting some kids' TV networks. Combined UK weekly TV share: 12.1% (third behind BBC, ITV); 6.9% including streaming (behind Netflix and YouTube).

It debuts with a SXSW music documentary and will draw future programming from Sundance, SXSW, Tribeca, TIFF, Cannes, Berlin and its own Fantastic Fest.

Specific Implication

CEO Summary
Comcast will split into two companies: a pure-play media company holding NBCUniversal and Sky (studios, IP, premium sports rights, parks) and a broadband, cable and wireless provider. The stock rose 4.5% Monday on four times normal volume, and Wolfe Research is already modeling a WBD-style bidding war for NBCU with Netflix expected to circle. Why This Matters
The one-year tax caveat is the load-bearing term. Because any transaction inside the first year of the split would forfeit its tax-free treatment, the structure establishes a knowable date after which the standalone media company can be sold without penalty. That gives prospective acquirers a fixed clock to prepare bids against, rather than an open question of whether NBCUniversal ever comes to market.
Specific Implication
Any output deal or windowing arrangement running through Universal or Peacock has a counterparty whose ownership structure is scheduled to change. Under the split's tax terms, a sale is not permitted until at least one year after separation.

Midjourney Moves to Force Disney, Universal and WBD to Disclose Their Own Internal AI Use
A magistrate judge already ruled the studios need disclose only "consumer-facing" AI tools, not internal ones. Midjourney is now asking a federal judge to overturn that limit.
CEO Summary
Midjourney asked a federal judge to overturn a ruling limiting discovery, seeking to force Disney, Universal and Warner Bros. to disclose their internal AI use, including training datasets, model weights, and board-level AI presentations. The three studios sued Midjourney for enabling infringement of their characters; Midjourney claims fair use and "unclean hands," arguing the studios train on unlicensed material themselves.
Why This Matters
The motion converts the plaintiffs' own conduct into the contested ground. If the "industry custom, even among the studios themselves" theory earns discovery, the studios' cost of prosecuting the case comes to include forced disclosure of their internal training practices. That exposure operates as settlement pressure independent of whether the unclean-hands defense would ultimately succeed on the merits.
The Facts
Midjourney filed a motion this week asking a federal judge to overturn a June ruling that limited its discovery, seeking to force Disney, Universal, and Warner Bros. to reveal how they use AI internally, including training datasets, model weights, research reports, and board-level AI presentations. The three studios sued Midjourney last year for enabling infringement of their copyrighted characters; Midjourney claims "fair use" and argues the studios engage in the same practices ("unclean hands"). A magistrate judge had ruled the studios need only disclose "consumer-facing" AI applications, not internal tools, calling the internal question irrelevant to whether Midjourney infringed. Midjourney argues that if the studios train image-generating models on unlicensed copyrighted data for storyboarding or ideation, that would show downloading/training on unlicensed content is "an industry custom, even among the studios themselves."

UK Regulator Enters the Paramount-WBD Merger Late, Putting a $650M-a-Quarter Clock in Play
Paramount owes WBD shareholders 25 cents a share, about $650 million, for every quarter the close slips past Q3. A UK phase-two referral could push closing into early 2027.
CEO Summary
The UK culture secretary said she is "minded" to intervene in Paramount's ~$110B acquisition of Warner Bros. Discovery; a formal notice would trigger 40-day Ofcom and CMA reviews, and a phase-two referral could push closing into early 2027. Paramount targets a September close to stop the ticking fee from accruing. The EU is expected to clear by July 22.
Why This Matters
The market-share figures undercut the substantive case for blocking. A combined entity ranking third in UK linear at roughly 12.1% behind the BBC and ITV, and holding roughly 6.9% with streaming counted, is not a plurality problem on the numbers. The UK process therefore threatens the deal through delay rather than denial. The ticking-fee structure converts that delay into direct leverage, because every quarter of review the regulator consumes carries a priced cost the acquirer alone bears.
Specific Implication
Counterparties with time-sensitive deals or renewals tied to either company should watch the UK's July 6 representation deadline and the phase-one/phase-two decision that follows. That decision determines whether the merger closes near September or slips into early 2027.
The Facts
The UK culture secretary said she is "minded" to intervene in Paramount's ~$110B acquisition of Warner Bros. Discovery, calling the deal "unprecedented." Paramount has until July 6 to make final representations; a formal intervention notice, considered the likely outcome, would trigger Ofcom and CMA "phase one" reviews (40 days). A clearance ends it in weeks; a referral to "phase two" could run 5+ months and push closing into early 2027. Paramount targets a September close because of the per-quarter "ticking fee" owed to WBD shareholders for slippage past Q3. In parallel, the EU appears set to clear the deal after Paramount's June 30 concessions (including exiting its UIP distribution JV with Universal), with an EU decision expected by July 22. Combined UK linear share would be ~12.1% (third behind BBC and ITV); including streaming, ~6.9%, behind Netflix and YouTube.
· Deadline · Jake Kanter · 2026-07-02 · https://deadline.com/2026/07/paramount-warner-merger-antitrust-crunch-time-europe-1236973099/ source ↗
A Major Theater Chain Forward-Integrates Into Distribution to Fill the Festival-to-Theatrical Gap
Alamo Exclusives launches with a SXSW music documentary, tickets on sale July 21, and plans to pull further titles from Sundance, Tribeca, TIFF, Cannes, Berlin and its own Fantastic Fest.
CEO Summary
Alamo Drafthouse is launching Alamo Exclusives, a distribution label giving unreleased festival titles limited theatrical runs on its own screens. It debuts with a SXSW music documentary and plans programming from Sundance, SXSW, Tribeca, TIFF, Cannes, Berlin and Fantastic Fest, working directly with filmmakers on presentation and marketing.
Why This Matters
An exhibitor stepping into the distributor's slot changes who prices the failure of the acquisition market. Limited runs confined to one chain's own screens provide exposure without a nationwide footprint or a standard P&A commitment. The label therefore functions less as a rival to a conventional distribution deal than as a floor beneath it. The existence of that floor marginally weakens a distributor's ability to present any theatrical release at all as a concession in negotiation.
Specific Implication
A festival film that fails to secure standard distribution now has a direct-to-venue theatrical option through this label.
The Facts
Alamo Drafthouse is launching a distribution label, Alamo Exclusives, offering limited-run theatrical engagements on its own screens to unreleased festival titles. The chain frames it as a response to the growing difficulty independently financed festival films face in finding nationwide theatrical distribution. It launches with a SXSW music documentary (tickets July 21) and plans future programming from Sundance, SXSW, Tribeca, TIFF, Cannes, Berlin, and its own Fantastic Fest. The chain works directly with filmmakers, providing presentation, marketing, and theatrical exposure.

Sony PlayStation to End Physical Game Disc Production by 2028 — Another Ownership-to-Access Domino Falls
US game consumer spend grew just 1 percent to $60.7 billion in 2025 while subscription revenue rose 20 percent, the math Sony cited for retiring the format it launched in 1994.
CEO Summary
Sony Interactive Entertainment will end production of new PlayStation game discs by January 2028, citing consumer preference for digital. The move routes every new game sale through the PlayStation Store; US game subscription revenue grew 20% in 2025 while total spend was roughly flat at $60.7B.
Why This Matters
The stated rationale, a "general preference for digital media," names the demand side and omits the supply-side prize. Retiring the disc eliminates used-game resale and the retail intermediary in one move, leaving every transaction inside a storefront where the platform holder sets the price and takes the margin. The format decision is best read as a move to full channel control that consumer preference makes politically costless.

Comcast to Split NBCUniversal & Sky Media From Technology Business
Why This Matters
Comcast's spinoff separates NBCUniversal's content and studio businesses from the connectivity business, a structure Warner Bros. Discovery adopted before its own sale process to Paramount Skydance. LightShed Partners' analysis states that a standalone NBCUniversal, without the connectivity business's balance sheet, has more capital and rationale to make acquisitions of its own, characterizing it as "a buyer, not a seller."
Specific Implication
Peacock is the only streaming asset inside the NBCUniversal spinoff; programming and packaging decisions affecting Peacock's engagement and retention carry direct weight on the value of the standalone company as structured.
The Facts
Comcast announced on June 29, 2026 that it will separate into two independent public companies via a tax-free spinoff expected to close in roughly one year. NBCUniversal — housing Universal film and TV studios, NBC, Telemundo, Peacock, Bravo, and the recently acquired Sky (which itself is pursuing a £1.6B bid for ITV) — will be run by co-CEO Mike Cavanagh. The remaining Comcast entity, focused on broadband, wireless and connectivity (65M+ homes/businesses), will be led by incoming CEO Michael Angelakis, Comcast's former CFO. Brian Roberts stays "actively involved" across both. Comcast retains up to 19.9% ownership in NBCUniversal/Sky for up to a year post-close. The move follows Comcast's earlier spinoff of most cable networks (CNBC, MS NOW, USA, Golf Channel, Oxygen, E!, Syfy) into Versant Media.

Per The Wall Street Journal, the deal is explicitly structured as R&D rather than content production, with A24 filmmakers working directly alongside DeepMind researchers. The announcement triggered immediate, widespread negative reaction online and within the industry. It follows recent high-profile departures from DeepMind's research team (Nobel laureate John Jumper, plus Jonas Adler and Alexander Pritzel, to Anthropic), which coincided with a roughly 5% drop in Alphabet shares.

'Project Hail Mary' Arriving On Prime Video After 105-Day Theatrical Window
Why This Matters
Amazon MGM applies two different release sequences depending on which part of the company greenlit the title: a studio-originated film goes theatrical, then PVOD, then MGM+, then Prime Video, while a Prime Video-greenlit title skips the MGM+ step. Project Hail Mary's 105-day theatrical window followed the studio-originated sequence, on a film that has grossed over $683 million worldwide.
Specific Implication
The originating division of a project inside Amazon MGM determines which release sequence and window length apply; that determination is made before the theatrical release, not based on the film's performance during its run.
The Facts
Amazon MGM Studios' theatrical hit will premiere on Prime Video on July 3, 2026 — a 105-day theatrical window. The film went through Amazon MGM's traditional windowing sequence (theatrical → PVOD → MGM+ pay-one window on June 18, a 90-day window → Prime Video). By contrast, a film greenlit directly by Prime Video (the studio's example: a Dwayne Johnson/Chris Evans feature) skips the MGM+ pay-one step and goes straight to Prime Video after theatrical. The film has grossed over $683M worldwide, the third-biggest global title of 2026 to date, and will be an awards-campaign priority for the studio this year.

Wilder. The recreation was produced by a named AI vendor, ElevenLabs, and used as a game element in an unscripted series rather than a scripted dramatic role.
Specific Implication
The estate-authorization structure used here, naming the AI vendor and securing explicit family sign-off with a public statement, is one documented approach to posthumous voice recreation; whether it satisfies requirements under the pending NO FAKES Act is not addressed in this reporting.
The Facts

Wilder). The show is produced by Eureka Productions in partnership with Netflix, which owns the underlying Roald Dahl Story Company (acquired 2021). Twelve contestant pairs will navigate Wonka-inspired physical/mental/moral challenges across nine episodes.

Getty Images Abandons $3.7 Billion Merger With Shutterstock
Why This Matters
The Getty-Shutterstock merger received unconditional US Department of Justice antitrust clearance in February 2026, but Getty's board terminated the deal after the UK's Competition and Markets Authority conditioned approval on a divestiture Getty's board deemed a non-starter. US antitrust clearance did not extend to UK regulatory approval on this transaction.
Specific Implication
A transaction that clears US antitrust review does not thereby clear UK Competition and Markets Authority review; a merger under active review in both jurisdictions carries the UK outcome as a separate, non-derivative risk.
The Facts
Getty Images's board unanimously terminated its proposed $3.7B merger with Shutterstock after the UK's Competition and Markets Authority (CMA) conditioned approval on Shutterstock divesting its editorial/celebrity/news photo business (Backgrid, Splash News, and related units) — a requirement Getty's board deemed a non-starter. The US Department of Justice had already granted unconditional antitrust clearance in February 2026. Shutterstock shares fell roughly 29-30% on the news. Getty faces a breakup fee and note-redemption obligations, and its board is now hiring a financial advisor to assess "strategic financing alternatives." Days before terminating the deal, Getty separately signed a licensing agreement with OpenAI for its image library across ChatGPT.
· The Hollywood Reporter (Erik Hayden) · 2026-06-30 · https://www.hollywoodreporter.com/business/business-news/getty-images-shutterstock-1236634242/ source ↗
